INTRODUCTION
This Part V-A of 1961 Census Report contains
special tables, on Scheduled Castes and Scheduled
Tribes covering the following topics:
(1) Workers and non-workers;
(2) Persons not at work:
(3) Cultivating households;
(4) Age and Marital status;
(5) Education;
(6) Religion;
(7) Mother tongue;
(8) Bilingualism.
In addition to the special tables, reprints from
earlier census publications on Scheduled Castes and
Scheduled Tribes are included in this book
Such special tables for Scheduled Castes and
Scheduled Tribes have never been published before.
At the 1951 Census a table was prepared giving
the total number of persons belonging to Scheduled
Castes and Scheduled Tribes and their distribution
among the eight livelihood classes adopted for
the Census. The constitution has provided special
safeguards for these communities and the Government are taking measures for the improvement of
the conditions of these communities by suitable
welfare schemes. The special tables prepared on
the basis of the 1961 Census data will help to evaluate the progress that has been made by the
members of the Scheduled Castes and Scheduled
Tribes through the schemes undertaken during the
past and to formulate schemes for the future.
Further, population figures for individual Schedule~ Caste and Scheduled Tribe are often required.

SpeCIal tables have, therefore, been devised to
give population figures for each Scheduled Caste
and Scheduled Tribe and their cross classification
by religion, age, marital status, education and
distribution by industrial categories as workers
and n?n-workers. 1961 Census population figures
collectIvely for all Scheduled Castes and similarly
for Scheduled Tribes, with their break-up into
males and females, for Himachal Pradesh its
administrative divisions, i.e., districts, ~hsilsf
Bub-tehsilsfsub-divisions and towns have been
presented in the Primary Census Abstracts printed
In Part 11-1\ of 1961 Census Report. Similar

figures for each village and ward of each town will
be found in the Village Directory printed in the
District Handbooks.
1961 Census Questionnaire

During population count tbe following two
Schedules were prepared:
1. Household Schedule-cum-Census Population ·Record.
2. Individual Slip (Enumeration Slip).
The Household Schedule was introduced for the
first time in the 1961 Oensus. Through it information on the extent of land cultivated by the household either owned or held from Government or
held from private persons or institutions for payment in money, kind or share, land given to private
persons for cultivation for payment in money,
kind or share, nature of Household Industry
conducted by the household and the duration of
the industry in a year along with number of family
workers and hired workers engaged in Cultivation
or Household Industry or in both were collected.
At the back of the Household Schedule there was
a Census Population Record as counted at the
Census in each household giving an abstract of
information for each individual enumerated in
the household.
In the Household Schedule, 'You will tind a
parallelogram at the right hand side again~t the
Full Name of the Head of the Household marked
"S.C:/S.T.". If the Head of the Household is a
member of the Scheduled Caste or Scheduled Tribe
obtaining in your State or in your District, you
should write the name of the particular Scheduled
Caste or Scheduled Tribe to which the Head of
the Household belongs within the parallelogram'.
In the Individual Slip (Enumeration Slip)
essential demographic data like relationship to
Head of Household, sex, age, marital status and
birth place, social and cultural data like nationality, religion, literacy and mother tongue and
economic data like occupation, industry, class of
worker, and activity if not working were also
collected.
In the Individual Slip, there was a specific
question to ascertain the name of the caste or tribe
of every person belonging to any of the Schedu!

v
Castes or Scheduled Tribes. The instructions to
enumerators were as follows:
'~Q. 5(0). Scheduled Castes & Scheduled T'I'ibesThe answer to this question will be recorded only
if a person belongs to a Scheduled Caste or Sche~
duled Tribe. If the person belongs to a Schedu1e~
Caste or Scheduled Tribe obtaining in your State, or
in your district write the name of the caste or tribe
to which he /she belongs. For all others, write 'X'
in this column. A list* of Scheduled Castes and
Scheduled Tribes obtaining in your,'8tate or in
your district is given elsewhere in these mstructions.
If the person belonging to a Scheduled Caste or
Scheduled Tribe returns his/her caste or tribe by
a synonym or, a generict name of a caste or tribe
as given in your list, write the name as return'ed
and the name ?f the relevant caste or tribe, af;! per
printed list, wjthin braykets.

Do no~ writihe names of Scheduled Castes in
general terms s· :Har~an', 'Achhut'. You should
ascertain the n~ e Oil ~he caste when it is returned
and write it. If'a peison knegligent and insists on
calling himself/hers~lf merely 'Harijan' tell him/her
that this description will not earn the person any
benefits under the Constitution permissible to
Scheduled Caste~ and Scheduled Tribes. This may
persuade him/her to give out the correct name.
Scheduled Castes can belong only to the Hindu
or Sikh religions. If aperson belongs to a Scheduled
Caste, there will be either 'H' or'S' in the answer
to question 5(b)t Scheduled Tribes may belong to
any religion."
The lists of Scheduled Castes and Scheduled
Tribes, as specified for Himachal Pradesh/union
Territory in the Government of India, Ministry of
Home Affairs, Scheduled Castes and Scheduled
Tribes Lists-Modification Order, 1956, were
provided to the enumerators alongwith the instructions for recording the entries about them. The
lists are printed as Annexure I to the Introduction
here. In the instructions it had been laid down that
if a pers~n belonging to a Scheduled Caste or Scheduled Tribe returned his/her Oaste or Tribe by a
synonym or generic name of the Oaste or Tribe
the name as returned was to be written and the
name of the relevant Caste or Tribe specified for
Himachal Pradesh was to be written in brackets.

* See Annexure I to the Introduotion.

t See Annexure II to the ,Introduction.
:1: In Question /5 (b), '1[' stood for lIindu &nd 'S' fQr Sikh.

List of specified Scheduled Castes which have
synonyms or generic names, which was supplied to
the enumerators, is printed as Annexure II to this
Introduction.
Information about synonyms or generic names
corresponding to a Scheduled Caste was .obtained
from District Oensus Officers and Charge Officers.
Data collected in reference to question 5(c) of
the Individual Slip was cross tabulated by answers
to other questions for preparation of the Tables
SOT-I, SOT-II, SOT-III, SOT-IV, -SO-I, ST-I and
ST-II. Table SOT-V has been prepared on a 20
per cent sample of households from Household
Schedules by mechanical tabulation. All other
tables have been prepared on full count from Individual Slips.
In the tables terms used, such as IndustrIal
Olassification of Workers, Non-Workers, Age,
Marital Status, Education, Religion, Household,
Cultivation, Interest in Land, Size of Land, besides
the terms Scheduled Oastes and Scheduled Tribes.
Definitions and explanations of the last two terms
have been given here in this Introduction. The
other terms will be explained in the respective
fly-leafs of the tables, where they come up for
discussion. In the fly-leafs scope and coverage of
the tables have been shown.

Presentation of Tables
Part V-A of 1961 Census Report is presented in
two sections. Section I contains special tables on
Scheduled Castes and Scheduled Tribes. Reprints
from earlier census publications on Scheduled
Oastes and Scheduled Tribes are presented in
Section II.

Coverage
The tables generally present the information for
administrative divisions upto the district level that
existed at the time of taking the Census, for rural
and urban areas separately. The exact coverage of
each table has, however, been specified in the
fly-leaf preceding it. The adninistrative divisions
are listed on the next page as also the towns (urban
areas). A map of Himachal Pradesh showin~ tbe
administrative divisions has been printed in tWs
publication.

PART II-HIMAOHAL PRADESH

THE SCHEDU,LED OASTES AND SCHEDULED TRIBES
LISTS (MODIFICA,TION) ORDER,

1956.

In pursuance of Section ~J of the States Reorganisation Act, 1956 (37 of 1956), and Section 14 of
the Bihar and West Bengal (Transfer of Territories)
Act, 1956 (40 of'J.956), the President hereby makes
the following Order, namely-

1. This Order may be called the Scheduled
Castes and Scheduled Tribes Lists (Modification)
Order, 1956.
2. (1) The Constitution (Scheduled Oastes) Order,
1950, is hereby modified in the manner and to
the extent specified in Schedule I.
(2) The Constitution (Scheduled Oastes) (Part
C States) Order, 1951, is hereby modified in the
manner and to the extent specified in Schedule II.
3. (1) The Constitution (Scheduled Tribes) Order,
1950, is hereby modified in the manner and to the
extent specified in Schedule III.

(2) The Constitution (Scheduled Tribes) (Part
C States) Order, 1951, is hereby modified in the
manner and to the extent specified in Schedule IV.

Definitions and Explanations
INDUSTRIAL CLASSIFICATION OF WORKERS (PERSONS AT WORK) AND NON-WORKERS

In the 1961 Cersus a.ccording to inshuction prescribed, a. worker was recorded workivg as a cultivator,
or as an agriclllturallabourer or at househ~ld industry or doing any other work, according to the work he did.
If be was workirg at more than one of the above categories, be was so recorded. In the case of a person
working a t hou~ehold industry, the nature of his work, the l'ature of the household industry and whether he
was an employee, were recorded.
For it persoD who was ,doi~g work other than as oultivator or as agricultural labourer or at household
industry, the nature of his, work, the Dature of industry, profession, trade or servioe in which he Wl:lS
employed, ,were recorded. In ilddition class of worker, whether he was an employer, employee, single worker
or fa,mily worker was :recorded. The name of the esta blishD.lent in which he was working was recorded.
In the case of thos~ who were not working, their activities were returned under the eight rategories
specified in the instructions.,
In 1961 Census, for economic classification of the population, the emphasis was la.id on work. All
those who worked, even if a.worker derived no income for himself, sucb as an apprentice without stipend or
wages, were workers, exception being an adult woman wbo was engaged in household duties but doing
'1;10 other productive wo:rk t\) augtI).ent the family resources. Even part-time workers sucb as women or
.,children, who wer&engaged in work, such as rice pounding for sale or wages, or in domestic services for wages
for others or IYlinding cat.tle br selling fire-wood or making and selling cowdung cakes or grass etc., or any
such work, were treated as workers. Work included not only actual work but effective supervision and direction of work. A person, even thougb deriving an income, but doing no productive work, such as a retired person receiving a pension and not employed again, or a rentier or a person living on agricu.ltural or non-agricultural royalty, rent or dividerd for securing which he did not have to work and who did no other work
was not a worker. He was a Don-worker.
I

Thus all tbe working population engaged in any branch 'of productive work are basically tenned as
'Workers' and 8011 others not engaged in any productive work as 'Non-workers' in 1961 Census.
In the data collected in the case of any person more than one/two productive activity/activities or
work/works could have been recorded. In sucb cases his principal and secondary works were clearly recorded,
during enumera.tion with the help of significant symbols. The principal work was the OLe on which be spent
the most time and the secondary work was t}:>e next in importance to his principal work.
WORKERS CLASSIFIED INTO NINE BROAD INDUSTRIAL CATEGORIES

The economic ciassific8tioD of the 1961 Oensus population in tables is based on the Indian Standa.rd
Industrial Classification aL d the National C)assificatior of Occupations, adopted by the Government ofIndia.
The industrial classifioation of the workers according to the different sectors of economic activities with which
they are connected, and the occupational classifications according to exact functiol.\s pe:rformed in those
sectors of ecopomic activities are presented in different ta bles. Primarily they have been classified or grouped
into the" following nine broad industrial categories : Oategory I-As Oultivator--This category includes cultivators of land. A person was considered working
as a cultivator if he or she was engaged either as employer, single worker, or family worker in (a) cultivation
ofland or supervision or direction of cultivation ofland owned or held f:rom Government aI,d (b) cultiva.tion
of land or supervision or direction of cultivation of la.nd beld from private persons or institutions for
payment in morey, kind or share.

2

Points of significance to note are that(i) an agricultural labourer was not a cu.ltivator (see Category II below);
(ii) a person engaged in fruit ,growing orohardry or plantations like tea, coffee, rubbE'r, cinchona.
and other medicinal plantations was not a cultivator;
(iii) a person who had given out his land to another person or persons for (Jultivation for money,
kind or share of OlOp and who did not eVen supervise or direct cultivation of land, was not a
(Jultivator.
Oategory II-As Agr£.()ultu~alLabourer-This.category includE's all those persons who worked in anotber
person's land only as labourers without exercising ary supervision or d~lection in cultivatior, for wages in
cash, kind or share such as share of produce.

Oategory III--In Mining, Quarrying, Lwestock, Forestry, F~shing, Huntiny and Plantation,;, Orchards and

Oategory IV-At Household Indnstry-A household industry was not to be on the scale of a registered
f8ctOry. It should have been conducted by the Head of the household himself and/or mainly rr.embers of
the household at home Or within the village in rural areas and only at home in urban areas. Main criterion
for a household industry was the participation of one or more members of a household in rural areas.
In the urban areas the industry should haVE been. conwed to the house. A household industry related to
production, processing, servicing, repairmg or making and selling of goods. It did not include professions
such as pleader or doctor or ba.rber or waterman or astrologer. Persons working at household industry,
as defined above, acre classifieo, ir> this catego:ry ()f workers.
Persons not working in their OWIl household industries but working as paid employees ir another person's
household industry are also included in this Category IV.

Oategory V-In Manufaoturing other than Household Industry--Persons working in the registered factories are inoluded in this category. It includes all persons engaged in processing, manufacture and repairing
not included in category IV.
Cateflory VI-In Construction-All persons engaged in construction and maintenarce of buildings includin.g erection, flooring, decorative constructions, electrica·} and sanitary installa.~ions are included ir this
category. It also includes persons engaged in construction and maintenance of roads, railways, bridge!',
tunnels, telegraph and telephor:e lines, water ways and water reservoirs snch as bund, embankments, dam,
canal, tank tubewells, weils.
Category VII-In Trade and Oommerce-All persons ergaged or employed in trade-retail or wholesale
are included in this category. In addition this includes persons employed or engaged in commercial aotivities
like importing and exporting of goods and commodities, real estate and properties, stoLk, shares and future
providents a.rd insurances, money lending indigenous, banking and simila.r type of financial operation,
auctioneering, distribution of motion piotures, and all other activities conn,ected with trade and commerce
not covered above, including hiring out of durable goods such as electric fans, micropholJe, rickshaw, etc.
CategOTy VIII-In Transport, Storage and Communications-All persons engaged or employed in any
frOID or mode of transport, storage and warehousing actIvities, and coID.Illu:r:ications suoh as postal, tele-

graphi(J, wireless, signa.l, telephone, are included in this category. Persons evgaged or employed in information and broadcasting too come under this category.

Oategory IX-In Other Sert'ices-Persons employed ill electricity, gas, water and sanitary services,
public services, educational and scientific services, medical and health servioes, religious and welfare services,

NON-WORKERS

All those persons who were not engag(jd in any productive activity have been treated in the light of
instructions prescribed 3S 'Non-workers' such as1. A full-¥me student or chiLd attending school who does no other work, such as make
article.s at home for sale, nor even help part-time in his own family cultivation, industry, trade
or bus~ess.
2. A person fDgaged in llllpaid hOiDe cluties (like h!)use-wife or othe.:. adult female) who does no other
wo:.:k, s~('h as make articles at home for sale or wages, nor help regularly even part-time in family.
cultivat1or, industry, trade or busines~.

3. Any depen~ant, inc.'luding an infant or child not at~nding school, a person permanertly disabled
from wolk bec~upe of illness or old age.
4. A retired pmson'~ho ifl not employed again, rentier, person living on agricultura.l or non-agricultural
royalty, rent or'dividend or any other person of independevt meaDS for securipg which he does
not have to work 1\rd who does no other work.
tl. A beggar, vagrant .or independent woman without indication of source of income and others of

unspecified source of existence.
6. A convict i:rl jail (:m undertrial prisoner will be shown as a worker if he llsed to work befOrE: he was
apprehended) or an inmate of a penal, mental or charitable irstitution.
7. A person who has not been employed before but is seeking employmert for the first time.
8. A person employed before but now out of employment and seeking employment.
SCOPE AND COVERAGE OF 'IRE TABLE SCT-I-PART A

This table has been prepared for each Scheduled Caste and separately for rural and urban popUlation
down to the district level. This is a very important table as it gives the population of each Scheduled Caste
separately for rural and urban areas and also shows ill which sector of Industry members of each Scheduled
Caste are working. In other words, the meJlJ.bers of each Scheduled Caste are shown classified. into nine broad
industrial categories of workers taking into account their principal works for classification.
The population figures of each individual, caste are often required for welfare and other work. The
table will be helpful in this respect.
The table also shows the number of persons belonging to each Scheduled Caste ellgaged in specialoccupation like (i) Tanning ard Currying of hides and skins and (ii) Scavenging. The workers engaged in
scavengirg occupation include those who clea.n and sweep offices of Government or institutions, household
tenements, roads, etc. The Dumber of such persons shown in the tabla includes sweepers (both dry and wet).

Definitions and Explanations
Education : In 1961 Census, a person who could neither read nor write or could merely read but could
not write in any language was considered illiterate. A person who could both read .and write with understanding was considered literate. In the case of a person who could both read and wrIte and had also passed
a written examiuatior or examinatioI's as proof of an educational standard attaired, the highest examinatim::: passed had been recorded.

to the district level.\ It ~ves the literacy and educational standards for each caste and tribe,

SCOPE AND COVERAGE OF THE TABLE SCT-IV PART A
The mble has been prepared for each Scheduled Caste for rural and urban areas separately down to
the district level. The members of Scheduled Castes will be either Hindus or Sikh.". The b.lhle is intended
~o give the religion professed by each Scheduled Caste.

Definitions and explanations
H0U8e~td : A household means the entire group of persons who commonly live together in a census
house and take their meals from a common mess unless the exigencies of work prevent them from doing so.

Head of Household : ThE;) Head of the household for census purposes is a person on whom falls the chief
responsibility for upe maintenance of the household. Thus the Head of the household need not neoessarily
be the eldest male )nember but may even be a. fe~ale or a younger member of either sex.

or held from governkent of ~eld from private persons or institutions for payntent iu money, kir d or share.

tNSTRUCTIONS TO ENUMERATORS
"You will find a parallelogram at the right-hand
side against the Name of the Head of the household
marked "RC./S.T.".

If the. Head of the Household is a member of
the Scheduled Caste or Scheduled Tribe obtaining
in your State or in your District, you should write
the name of the particular Scheduled Caste or
Scheduled Tribe to which the Head of the Household belongs within the parallelogram.
You should then fill up the part of the household
schedule relating to Cultivation and Household
Ip.dustry. Sub-part 'A' relates to Cultivation; subpart 'B' to Household Industry and sub-part 'c'
to Workers at Cultivation or Household Industry.
Sub-part 'A' relating to Cultivation will have
to·be filled in only where the household cultivates
land (Land includes all land normally used for
cultivation purposes ipcluding temporary fallows).
Three categories of land are given in items I (i),
I(ii) and 2. Items I(i) and I(ii) relate to land
actually cultivated by the household. Item 2
relates to land which is not cultivated by the
household but has been given by it to private
persons for cultivation for
payment in
money, kind or share. In each of the three cases
the total of separate plots or parcels of land in
different places, owned or held, or taken or given
should be made and entered. A number of lines
has been provided for this purpose in I( i)and I( ii)
to account for separate plots or parcels of land
held or owned or taken under different recognised
local rights. If the household (a) cultivates land
owned or held from Government, or taken from
private persons or institutions or (~) has given
land to private persons for cultivation purposes
you should ascertain the local name of the right
on such-land and record in the column relating to
'local name of right on land '. Category I(i)
land owned or held from Govermnent, will include the total of all pieces of land owned or held
in owner-like possession, e.g., land held directly
from Government under a. grant, lease or assignment, (i) with rights of permanent, heritable and'
tr8Jl!ferable po~ession, (ii). with righu of permanent
and heritable possession, but without the right of
transfer and (iii) temporary or ,(onditional leases
of any kind with the-Government. Category I(i~)
land taken from private persons or institutions for
payment in money, kind or share, will inculde
land (i) with rights of permanent, heritable and
1;J'a.z¢~r~t>le :P~Qll; (ii) with righ~ oipenna.nent

236
and heritable possession but without right of
transfer; (iii) held in variety of tenancies or tenurea
which may be broadly classified as follows:

(a) tenants holding land with permanent and·
heritable rights whose land cannot be
reBlllned by the owner on grounds of
personal cultivation (such tenants may
in some cases have the right of transfer
also);
(b) tenants who have been given permanent
rigJ;lts subject to the right of resumption
by the owner (in some cases the tenant
has the right to acquire ownership of the
non-resumable area. In other cases he
does not posse-s the right);

(0) tenants holding land in areas where interipl
measures have been enacted for stay of
ejectment or for continuing the leaseM for
a specified period;
'\
(d) tenants holding land on temporary ·leases
who are liable to ejectment; and
(e) areas held on condition of rendering ~ervice
either to a village community or to Govern-

ment. (This also includes cases where
labourers on plantations are given some
land for cultivation with permanent
rights); and
(iv) The following .-

(a) land taken for afixed_amount-of money,
(b) land taken for a:fixed amount of produce,
(c) land taken for a share of the produce.
'(d) land for which money is paid partly in
one and partly in any other form ment:oned above, and
(e) land held free of consideration.

. Category 2 Land given to private. persons for
cultivation for payment in money, kmd or share
will include the classifications mentioned in category I(ii).
Categories l(i) and I(ii) will include only those
plots or parcels of land whIch are cultivated by the
household itself, that is, lands which lie within the
Village, or in adjacent villages, or within such a,
distance as enables the household to work on the
land or actively supervise the cultivation. They
will not include land owned or held in distant p:aces
where distance itself is a bar to active cultivation,
constant supervision or'direction. But Category 2

TABLE seT-IV l»ART B

237
will include land in any part of the country what.soever. You should ascertain the extent of land in
each category of local rights in acres and record
in the column relating to 'Area in acres'. You should
write the total of separate plots or parcels of land
under the same right if they are situated in different places. If it is not possible to ascertain the
extent of land in acres you should, if possible,
make a conversion from the local measure given
to you. Even if this is not possible write the area
in local measure giving invariably the name of the
measure adopted.
This sub-part will not apply to households which
do not possess any land. Thus, it will not apply to
households which consist of only agricultural
labourers without any land. In s11.ch cases put an
'X' in all the spaces provided in the right hand
\
side and put a bold cross on this sub-part.

:A'

Land under ani, of the ite~s in .sub-par~
includes rent' free land, lands enJoyed ill perIDlSslve
possession or gifts Wt'thou~ enc_umbrance or conside.ration. A househo which IS a member of a cooperative farm w' record .only that amount of
land in the co-operative farm which had belo.Ll.~(d to
it before and under rigl;lt enter '<lo-operative farm'.
Labour hirea by surh a co-operative farm should
~ot be entered for thlR household."
The terms 'land' and 'cultivation' appearing in
the instructions to enumerators are elucida~d
further for fuller understanding of their concepts.
Land here means land normally used for cultt
vation purposes including temporary fallows.
Cultivation involves ploughing, sowing and harvesting and does not include fruit growing or keeping orchards or groves or working for plantations like
tea, coffee, rubber, cinchona and other medicinal
plantations. So in reference to a land lillder cultivation by household or land given to private
person(s) for cultivation, only that land is covered
which involved ploughing, sowing and harvesting.
The following classification illustrates as to which
land comes under th6 definition of cultivation
and which not.

Oultivations involving pkJuging, sO'IJlI,ng and
harvesting
(1) Production of cereal crops (including
Bengal gram), such as rice, wheat, jowar,
bajra, maize; roots and tubers like
'potato, yam, beet etc; sugarcane etc.
(2) Production of pulses, such asaroor, nwong,
masur, urd, khesari, other gram.

(3) Produotion of raw jute and kindred fibre
crops.
(4) Production of raw cotton and kindred
fibre crops.

Other Field produce not covered by the definition
of Oultivation
(1) Production of other crops including
vegetables not covered under cultivation.
(2) Production of fruits and nuts in plantations, vines and orchards (like coconut,
arecanut etc.)
(3) Production of wood, bamboo, cane reeds,
thatching grass etc.
(4) Production of juice by tapping paJms.
(5) Production of other agriCUltural produce
(including fruits and nuts not covered
by 81. No. (2) and flowers) not covered
above.

Plantation Orops not covered by the definition
of Oultivation
(1) Production Qf tea. in plantation.
(2) Production of coffee in plantation.
(3) Production of rubber in plantation.
(4) Production of tobacco in plantation (does
not include tobacco which is cultivated
in rotation with other cereal crops, in
which case it would go under cultivation),
(5) Production of ganja, cinchona, opium,
poppy, saffron etc.
(6) Production of oth~r plantation crops
not covered above.
Size of Lana Oultivated: The extent of
land under cultivation has been ascertained
from the household and recorded in acres. Where,
however, the area could not be obtained in acres
at all satisfactorily, the area. in local meaaureshas
been ascertained and with the help of a conversion
table recorded in acres.

under two legends, viz., (i) owned or held from

il;lstitutions for pa.yment in money, kind or share.

be classified into the following three categories.

(0) Partly held. from Government and partly
from private persons or institutions for
payment in money, kind or share.

Symbols (a), (b) and (0) are printed in place
of the above three categories of interest ,in land
cultivated under column 1 of the table..
The table will give very useful information in
regard to sizes of holdings of SGheduled Castes.

The table has been p~911ared for total Scheduled Castes and Dot for individual castes. The presentation
is for Himachal Pradesh and its six districts and urder each of the five districts of Chamba, Mandi, Bilaspur,
Mahasu and SirmUf for total, ruraJ and urban areas separately. For KiDDaur Djstrict the presentation is for
total or say rural area as there is no urban area in this district.
The total non-working populatior of the Scheduled Castes is shoWl' in columns 2-4 of the table
males and fema.les separately. Out of them, the full time students are shown separately in coluIDDs 5 and 6.
The persons seeking employment/work among the Scheduled Castes are shown in columns 7-10. The other
non-workers are shown separa-tely in columns 11 and 12. All of them have further been classified according
to broad educational standard or levels, normally of literates, Primary or Junior Basic, Matriculation or
Higher Secondary and above MatriclJlation or Higher Secondary.

REPRINTS
whips and other leather work; and above all they
do an immense deal of hard work in the fields,
each family supplying each cultivating association
with the continuous labour of a certain number of
hands. All this they do as village menials, regeiving
fixed customary dues in the shape of a share of the
produce of the fields. In the east and south-east
of the Punjab the village Cbamars also do a great
deal of weaving, which however is paid for separately: The Chamars stand far above the Chuhras
in social position, and some of their tribes are
almost accepted as Hindustt. They are generally
dark in colour, and are almost certainly of abori·
ginal origin, though here again thelr numbers have
perhaps been swollen by members of other and
higher castes who have fallen or been degraded.
The people Say:
Karia Brahman, got Chamar
In ke sath na utrie par.
"Do not cross the ferry with a black Brahman
or a fair Chamar", one being as unusual as the
other. Their women are celebrated for beauty,
and loss of caste is often attributed to too great
partiality for a Chamari.
The traditional origin of the Chamars is that
Chanu (or Chanwe) and Banu were two brothers,
the former removed a cow's carcase with his
own hands and so Banut out-casted him§. In
Kapurthala, however, another version is current
and"" aocording to this Gat told his brother Mat
to remove a oarcase and then declined to associate
with him for doing so, and ·the Mirasi who witnessed
the incident, took Gat's part. From Mat are
descended the Chamars.
Synonyms: It is difficult to s:;ty that what are
the real synonyms of Chamar. The term ChuhraOhamar is often used to denote the group formed
by the two castes, just as Mochi-Julaha is used,
but it does not imply that the two oastes are identical. Just as the Muhammadan Chamar is
styled Mochi so the Sikh Chamar is called Ramdasia (qq.v.). In Sirsa Cha~ar is called Meghwal as a compliment, but opprobriously he is
styled Dhedll or Dherh, a term applied to any
'low fellow'. The 'Meghwal' claim descent from
Megh-rikh who was created by Narain.

Extract from "A Glossary of the Tribes and Oastes
of the Punjab and North West Frontier Provinoe"
by H. A. Rose, Volume II (p. 32, 64 and 65)
Badhi: The carpenter who makes ploughs and
other rude wood-work among the Gaddis: (fr.
badhna, to cut with an axe or saw). See Barhi.
Barhai: A wood-cutter or carpenter in the hills
(root badhna, to cut, d. Badhi). In Kullu the
Barhais and Badhis are the same, but not in
Kangra. proper. In Kullu they do not scruple to
eat the flesh of dead animals. The Barhais are not
a. separate caste, but Kelis or Dagis that use the
axe, and one of the Koli groups is returned as
Barhai. There is also a Barhai tribe or clan among
the Rathis of Kangra.
Barhi: The synonym for Tarkhan in the Jumna
Districts. The Barhi considers himself superiOJ, to
his western brot~er the Khati, and will not marry
with him: his m_~rried women wear the nose ring.

Ohamar, Ohamiar, fem. Ghamari,-iari: The
Chamar is the tanner and leather-worker of NorthWestern India"', and in the western parts of the
Punjab he is called Mochi whenever he is, as he
generally is, a Musalman, the caste being one and
the same. The name Chamar is derived from the
Sanskrit Charmakara or "worker in hides". But
in the east of the Punjab he is far more than a
leather-worker. He is the general coolie and field
labourer of the villages; and a Chamar, if asked his
caste by an Englishman at any rate, will answer
"coolie" as often as "Chamar"t. They do all the
begar, or such work as cutting grass, carrying wood
and bundles, acting as watchmen, and the like;
and they plaster the houses with mud when they
need it. They take the hides of all dead cattle and
the flesh of all clovenfooted animals, that of such
as do not divide the hoof going to Chuhras. They
make and mend shoes, thongs for the cart, and

The Chamars are by religion Hindqs or Silms.
Owing to the fact that the famous Bhagat Ramdas
was a Chamar by caste, many Chamars are Ramdasiast by sect, and of this sect again some are
also Sikhs.
RamdaS' was a descendant of Chanu. His mother,
Kalsia was childless, but one day a faqir came to
her and she give him flour, in return for which
he promised her a son. On his return his guru
cross-questioned ~, as he was unable to prcnounce
the name 'Parmeshwar' and learning of his promise
declared that, as no son had been bestowed on
Kalsia in her destiny, the faqir himself must be
born to ·her. So he was reborn as Ramdas, who
is called Raidas in Bawal. As his mother was a
Chamari he refused her breasts, until his guru bade
him suck. One day when placed by his mother
at a epot where Rama Nand used to pass, he was

* The two most numerous gots among the l\1ochis.

touched by that teacher's sandals and when he
cried out was told by him to be silent and repea t
"Ram Ram". Thus was supernatural power
bestowed upon him.
Contrary to the Chamars' customs R~mdas
wore a janeo, sounded a conch, and worshIpped
'idols. The Brahmans appealed to the magistrate,
whereupon Ramdas cast the idols into a tank,
but they returned to him, whereas the Brahmans
failed in a similar test. Again, cutting his neck
open Ramdas exhibited 4 janeos, of gold, silver,
copper and thread, typical of the 4 yugas. Thenceforth he was known as a famous Bhagatt.
Chamar women wear no nose-ring, but among
the Bunas it is worn by married women 1not by
widows. The Charimars of Bawal do not wear
gold nose-rings and all the Chama-rs of that locality
avoid clothes dyed in saffron, and the use of gold.
They also use beestings only after offering it to the
gods 0]1 the amawas.

loosely used with several different meanings.

but it is more usually applied to a Ohamar or
Jullaha who has taken the pabul. It is generally
explained that this Guru first admitted. Ohamars

Ramdasia: According to Ibbetson in the north

~fligion. These men are called Ramdasia after

only Nanakpanthi Sikhs and do not take the pahul;
and. are followers of Bhagat Rav Das or Rab
Das, himself a Ohamar. They are appar~ntly as
true Hindus as any Ohamars can be, and are
wrongly called Sikhs by confusion with the Remdasias.

Extract from "The Oensus of India, 1901, India,

1. Origin : The caste of currier~, tanners, and
day-labourers found throughout Upper India.
Their name is derived from the Sanskrit Oharmakara, a "worker in leather." Traditionally the
Chamar is the offspring of a Chand ala woman by
a. man of the fisherman caste. The Karavara of
Manu, "who cuts leather", is descended from a
Nishada father and Vaideha mother. The Nishada,
again, is said to be the child of a Brahman and a
Sudra wom,an, and the Vaideha.of a Vaisya father
and a Brahman moth~r. On this Mr. Serring
remarks:-"If the workers in leather of the present
day are lineal descendants of the workers in leather
in Manu's time, the Chamars may fairly consider
themselves as of no mean degree and may hold up
their heads boldly in the presence of the higher
castes." Mr. Sherring appears to have been impressed with the high-bred appearance of some
Chamars. This may, perhaps, be to some extent
accounted for by liaisons with some of the higher
castes; but most observers will agree that Mr.
Risley is right ~ his opinion, that "the average
Chamar is hardly distinguishable in point of
features, stature, or complexion from the members
of those non-Aryan races from whose ranks we
should prima facie expect the profession of leatherdressers to be recruited." Mr. Nesfield believes the
Chamar to have sprung out· of several different
tribes like the Dom, Kanjar, Habura, Chero, etc.,
the last remains of which are still outside the pale
of Hindu society. "Originally he seems to have
been an impressed labourer (begar) who was
made to hold the plough for his master, and
received in return space to ,build his mud hut near
the village, a fixed allowance of grain for every
working day, the free use of wood and grass on the
village lands, and the skins and bodies of the ani*But they were made of wood and called padukllo.

mals that died. This is very much the status of the
Chamar at .the present day. He is still the field
slave, the gJ;ass cutter, and the carrio.n-cater of
the Indian village." But it is, perhaps, at present,
until the existing evidence from anthropometry
is largely increased, premature to express a decided·
opinioI} of their origin further than this, that the
tribe is in all probability occupational, and largely
recruited from non-Aryan elements. Among all the
Indo-Aryan races the use of hides for clothing
prevailed in primitive times. The Vishnu Purana
enjoins all who wish to protect their persons never
to be without 'leather shoes; and Manu warns the
Brahmans never to use shoes that have been worn
by another. In the Ramayana, Bharata places on
the vacant throne of Ajudhya a pair of Rama's
slippers* and worships them during his exile.
The Charmal of plinY's list have been identified
with the inhabitants of Charma Mandala, a district of the West, mentioned in the Mahabharat,
and also in the Vishnu Purana under the title of
Charma-Khanda.
'\

2. Traditions of Origin: One curious legend of
the origin of the tribe has been referred to in con ~
nection with the Agarwala Banyas:\
Once upon a time a certain Raja had two dau~
ghters" Chamu and Bamu. These married, and each
gave birth to a son who was a prodigy of strength
(Pahlwan). An elephant happened to die in.... the
Raja's palace, and being unwilling that it should
be cut up, he searched for a man strong enough to
take it oup whole and bury ':it. Chamu undertook
and performed the task. Bamu pronounced him an
outcaste; so the Banyas are sprung from Bamu,
and the ChaIil"trs from Chamu. Another legend
tells ltow five Brahman brothers )Vere passing along
together. They saw a carcass of a cow lying on
the way. Fo~r of them turned l;tside; but the fifth
removed the dead body. His brethren excommunicated him, and since then it has been the business
of his descendants to remove the carcasses of cattle.
Another tradition makes them out to be the
descendan,ts of Nona or Lona Chamarin, who is
a defied witch much dreaded in the eastern part of
the Province. Her legend tells how Dhanwantari,
the physician of the godR was bitten by Takshka,
the king of the snakes, ana knowing that death
approached, he ordered his son to cook and eat his
body after his death, so that they might thereby
inherit his skill in medicine. They accordingly
cooked his body in a cauldronl and were about,to

289
~at it, when Takshaka appeared to them in the
form of a Brahman, and warned them against tbis
act of cannibalism. So they let the cauldron float
down the Ganges, and as it iloateo. down, Lona, the
Ohamarin, who was washing on the bank of the
river, not knowing that the vessel contained
human flesh, took it out and partook of the ghastly
food. She at once obtained pow!3r to cure diseases,
and especially snakebite. One day all the women
were transplanting rice, and it was found that
Lona could do as much work af:' all her companions
put together. So they watched her, and when she
thought she was alone she stripped off all her
clothes (nudity being an essential element in all
magic), muttered EOme spells, and throwing the
pJants into the air they all settled down in their
proper places. Finding she was observed1\ she
tried to escape, and as she ran the earth opened,
and all the w~ter of the rice field8 followed her,
and thus was formed the channel of the Loni river
in the Unao District.

4. Sub-Castes: In the detaileci lists we find the
Chamars of the province classified into sixteen
main sub-castes. Aharwar (principally found in
the Allahabad. Division), Chamar (chiefly
in
Meerut); Ohamkatiyas (mostly in Bareilly); DhllSiyas (in Meerut and Benares); Dohars (in Agra,
Rohilkhand, Allahabad, Lucknow); _ Gole (in
Etawah); JaiRwaras (Strongest in Benares, Allahabad, Gorakhpur llL.d Faizabad); J atwas (in,Meerut,
Agra and Rohilkhand); Koris (in Faizabad and
Gorakhpur); Korchamras (in Lucknow); Kurilf

290
(in Lucknow and Allahabad); Nigoti (a EmaIl subcaste chiefly in Manipur); Patthargotis (in Agra)g
Purabiyas (in Lucknow and Faizabad); Raedasis
(tolprably evenly distributeo throughout the
proVince) and Sakarwars (in Agra and Allahabad).
But there is hardly a district which does not
possess, or pretend to possess, the sevenfold
division which is so characteristic of castes of this
social standing. Thus in BaHia, we find Dhusiyas,
JaiBwaraB, KanaujiyaB, JhoghiyaB, JatuaB, Ohamartantoes, and Nonas; in Agra, Mathuriya,
Jadua, Domara, Sakarwar, Batariya, Guliya,
and Chandauriya. Some of these sub-castes are of
local origin, some are occupational, and some take
their name from their eponymous founder. Thus
the Aharwar are connected with the old town of
Ahar, in the Bulandshahr District, or with the
Ahar tribe; the Chamkatiyas take their llame
from their trade of cutting hides (ch51m katna).
Thus ,sub-caste claims to have produced the saints
Rae and Das Lona Chamarin. The J atua or J atiya
have, it is said, some unexplained connection with
the tribe of J ats. The Kaiyan is also a sub-caste
of the Bohras, and is said to be' derived from their
habit of always saying kahiL, "What?" "When?"
The Jaiswaras trace their origin to the old town
of Jais, though some 'have a ridiculous story that it
is a corruption of Jinswar in the sense that they are
agriculturists and grow various crops (jins). The
Koli, or Kori, a term usually applied to the Hindu
weaver, as contrasted with the J ulaha 9r Muhammadan weaver, are connected by some with the Kols;
by other!;) with the Sanskrit Kaulika, in the sense
of "ancestral" or a "weaver". Tlley say themselves
that- they take their name from their custom of
weaving unbleached (kora) clothes. The Jhusiya,
and also perhaps the Dhusiyas, have traditions
connecting them with the old town of Jhusi, near
Allahabad. There are, again, the Azamgarhiya of
Azamgarh; the Jatlot of Rohilkhand, who, like
the Jatiya, say they-are kinsfolk of the Jats; the
Sakarwar connect themselves witl;t Fatehpur
Sikri; in the Central Duab are the Saksena, who
say they come from Sankisa, and the Chanderiya
from Chanderi. In Mirzapur we :find the Jaiswara,
Jhusiya, Kanaujiya, Kur1a, Dusadhu, Kinsmen of
tbe Dhueadha, the Kori, the Mangta or "beggars,"
the Dolidhauwa or "palanquin-carriers," the
Azamgarhiya, and the Banaudhiya, who are
residents of Banaudha-a .term which includes
the western parts of Jaunpur, Azamgarh and
Benares, and the south of Oudh. To these Mr.
Sherring adds;-In Benares the Rangua (rang,
"colour") who are dyers; the Katyua or "cutters"

According to the same authority some of these

Chandauriya, of the Central Duab, claim to be
descended from Chanura the famous wrestler of

5. Rules oj Exogamy: These sub-castes are now
all, or practically all, endogamous; but there seems
reason for believing that this fission into endogamous groups may be comparatively recent.
Thus there seems no reason to doubt that in the
east of the Province the Dhusiya and Kanaujiya
intermarry. The rule of exogamy within the subcaste seems to vary. Those who are more advanced
say that marriage is proliibited within seven degrees
in the descending line. Others say that they do
not intermarry as long as any previous relationship
between the parties is known or ascertainable. In
BaHia, a careful observer state~ that they do not
marry in a family from which their mother, grandmother, or great grandmother has come, nor do
they marry in the family of their pa:r;e:{lt's sister.
A man may marry two sisters, but not. a daughter
of a brother-in-law. The descendffpts of one
common stock are called dayad, and among them
marriage is prohibited. Besides, this occupation
plays a very important part in marriage alliances;

291
thus, those who remove manure or night-soil
cannot intermarry with those who praotise the
cleanlier duty of horse-ke~ping. As a rule they
marry locally within their own neighbourhood, if a.
suitable match can be so arranged. If a Chamar
entice away the wife of a clansman, in addition
to the punishment inflicted by the tribal council,
he is obliged to repay her marriage expense. If a
girl is detected in an intrigne with a caste-fellow,
her parents are fined one and a quarter rupees, and
in Mirzapur the same is the punishment inflicted
on a man who marries again while his fir&t wife is
alive. In fact, polygamy is discouraged unless the
first wife be barren, when a second marriage will
usually bc sanctioned by the council. Among this
sort of quarrelling has the special !fame sautya dah
-"the ill-will-betwe~n the co-w,ives." Other say
to the same purport \ are "Kat~ ki saut bhi buri
boti hai".-"Even a eo-wife of wood is an evil",
and when one wife is being carried to the burningground, the other sa:w;:-"Mor jiya na patiawe;
saut ka pair hilta jawe\"-"r cannot believe that
she is dead; I am sure her' leis a.re shaking still."
In BaHia it is said that if a CJ;l_amar marries a secon.d
time, the fiest wife usu~lly l~ave~ him, and tha.t per
desertion for this reason is reoognised as aooording
to tribal custom.

6. Tribal Counoil : Chamars have a particqlarly
well-organised and influential tribal councilor
panchayat. The head of every family is supposed
to be a member of the Panchayat, and nearly
every village has a headman (pradhan, jamadar).
In large towns there is often more than one headman. In small matters the village council is competent to decide; but for the settlement of weightier
questions the councils of several villages assemble
under their own headmen, and then a general meeting is formed. Custom varies as to whether the
headman is a permanent official or not. The most
usual rule is that, if the son of the late headman
is competent, he is generally appointed; if he be
found guilty of misconduct, the headman is as liable
as any of the members to fine and excommunication.
The oases which come before the council may be
classified as (a) cases of illicit sexual relations or
violation of tribal rules concerning food, etc.,
(b) Matrimonial disputes; (c) petty quarrels, whioh
would not come under the cognizance of a Court;
(d) disputes about small money transactions; (e)
cases in connection w.ith jajmani: this last is very
common. Every Chamar family has assigned to it a
certain number of families of higher caste, which
are known as its jajman (Sans. Yajamana),

for which its members perform the duties of cutting
the cord at births, playing the drum at marriages
and other festive occasions, removing and disposing
of the carcasses of dead cattle, and in return for,
these services they receive money fees, cooked food
and sometimes grain, flour etc. In return they sometimes supply shoes at marriages, a certain number
of shoes annually in proportion to the hides they
receive, and also do repairs to leather articles such
as well buckets used in cultivation. These rights
are very jealously watched, and any interference
with the recognised constituents of a family is
strongly resented and brought before the tribal
council. These orders of the council iI\ the way of
fine or entertainment of the ciansmen are enforced
under penalty of excommunication, of which the
most serious result is that, until the ban is removed,
all marriage alliances with the family of the offender are barred, and if anyone marries a member of
such a family, he at once becomes liable to the
same punishment as that which tJ;wy are undergoing. Every council has a mace-bearer (chharidar),
who goes round and calls the members to the meetings and he is allowed a small monay fee for this
service. The amount of fine varies from one to five
rupees, and it is very seldom that the process of
excommunication has to be used to enforce payment. If a person thinks fit to lay a charge before
the council he has to pay a fee o~ one and quarter
rupees to the chairman, who will not take up the
case until the fee is paid. This money, which to the
east of the Province is known as nalbandi or lehri,
is spent in purchasing spirits for the refreshment of
the members.

7. Marriage : Chamars show an increasing
tendency to the adoption of infant-marriage. The
usual age to the east of the Province is between
four and eight, and it is not uncommon in BaHia
for little girls of three to be married. It is very
seldom that a girl remains unmarried after the age
of eight. There are no regular marriage brokers
employed; the negotiations are conducted by a
member of the family who is known as agua. As
among other Hindu ::.:astes, marriage is looked upon
as a sacrament, and not based on contract. It is
complete and binding once the prescribed ceremonies are gone through, and its validity does not
depend on the express or implied consent of the
parties. But no marriage is carried out without the
consent of all the relations, even those who are
distant, and the descent and family connections
of both bride and bride-groom are carefully

292
enquired into before the engagement is made. In
MiJ'~a.pur the bride-price payable to her relations is

two rupees and five seers of coarse ,sugar. In BaHia
they deny that there is a bride-price; but it is admitted that, if the parents of the bride are very
poor, the father of the bride-groom may give as
much as four rupees to defray the m,arriage expenses. As has been said, both bride and bridegroom are carefully examined as to whether they
are free from any physical defect, and, as a genera,l
rule, if such be subsequently ascertained, it would
not be a valid ground for annulling the marriage.
If the husband become a lunatic after marriage,
the wife in BaHia would not be entitl,ed to leave him,
provided his relations continued to support her:
and in the same way the husband of a mad wife is
held bound to support her. Impotence or such
mutilation·as renders sexual intercourse impossible
is valid ground for dissolving the marriage. But
as a'matter of fact, impotency, proved to the satisfaction ofthe council, is the only valid reason for a
wife abandoning her husband. Divorce in the strict
sense of the term is unknown; but a husband may
turn his wife out of the house for proved infidelity,
while she cannot leave him even if he be unfaithful
to her provided he gives her food and clothes. A
woman, whose expulsion has been recognised by
the council, can remarry by the sagai or karao form.
The offspring of such informal marriages rank
equally for purpose of inheritance with those of
regularly-married virgin brides. As regards the
offspring of illicit connections they follow the caste
and tribe of the father unless the mother was a
Musalman, or of some tribe lower than a Chamar
in the social scale. Such people are known by the
name of Suratwal or Suratwala. When a Chamar
takes a woman from a caste superior to his own,
their children will be recognised as members of the
caste; but if she be iDferior to him, their children
are considered illegitimate, and will not inherit.
This is always the case when the woman is a Bhangi,
Dom, Dhabi, Kunchbandhua, or Musahar.
The child of a Chamar at Ballia by a Dusadh
woman is known as Chamar Dusadha, and this is
the only case in which a similar fusion of castes is
known to have been recognised. The importance
of such facts in connection with the problem of the
origin of the mixed castes is obvious.

8. Widow-Marriage and the Levirate: Widowmarriage is, as has been said, fully recognised;
but among Chamars who have~ like those at Cawnpore, risen in the world, there seems a tendency to
prohibit it. The levirate is recognised, but the widow

R~:PRINTS

can live only with the younger brother of her
late husband. If the widow be younger, and her
younger brother-in-law of a suitable age, they usually arrange to live together; if this cannot be
arranged, she usually marries some widower of the
tribe by the sagai or karao' form. In this case the
brother and father of her late husband have a right
to the custody of thc children of the first marriage:
this rule is relaxed in the case of a baby, which
aooompanies its mother. In some oases the widow
is allowed to take with her to her new home all the
children of the first marriage. Any dispute as to
matters of this sort is settled by the tribal council.
If a widow marry an outsider, she Joses all claim
to the estate of her first husband" and so do any
children she takes with her to the house of her new
husband.
In such cases the property passes to the brothers
of-her first husband. If, on the 'contrary, she marry
her husband's brother, she or her husband will
inherit only if there was no mare, heir by the
first marriage. At ~he same time, though Chamars
are quite ready to lay down definite rules on this
subject, the tribal custom does not appear to be
quite settled, and when there are in the case of
levirate or widow-marriage, two families, the
matter is usually left to the council, who make
a partition.

9, Birth Ceremonies: Among some branches of
the tribe, as, for instancc, at Sultanpur, when the
first pregnancy of a wife is announced, a ceremony
known as,sathai is pl'rformed, which consists of the
distribution of cakes (puri) to the clansmen at
their houses. But as Chamars are particularly exposed to fear of witchcraft and diabolical agency
I generally, careful precautions are taken to guard
the woman from evil. To 'the east of the Province
promises of offerings are made to Vindhybasini
Devi of Bindhachal, Banrn Bir, Birtiya, and to the
sainted dead of the family if they vouchsafe an
easy delivery. Thorny ,branches of the bel tree
(Aegle marmelos) are hung at the door of the
delivery room to intercept evil spirits, who are
also scared away ,by the smoke from an old shoe,
whiclr is burnt for the purpose. The woman sits
on her heels during adcounchemcnt, and'is supported by her female relatives. She is attended by a
woman of the caste for six or twelve day~; which is
the period for impurity. When it is announced that
the child is a boy, the women sing the sohar or
song of rejoicing. Much of this consists of 'the invocation of Mata, the goddess of small-pox. After
the cord is cut, if the child be a boy, the mother

ImPRINTS

is bathed in warm water; if a girl, she gets a cold
bath. Mter th~ mother and baby are bathed,
she 'gets a meal consisting of molasses, turmeric,
and oil, and after twelve hours she is given halwa
sweetmeat. Next day'she gets her ordinary food.
All through the period of impurity the signing of
the sohar is repeated. At the door of the delivery
room (sauri; Sans. Sutaka) a fire is kept constantly
burning, and into it some ajwain (linguasticum
ajowan) is occasionally thrown. At least for the
first six days \a light is kept constantly burning.
On the night of the sixth day the women sit up all
night and worship Shashti or Chhathi, the goddess
of the sixth, with an offering of cakes made of
barley-flour and rice boiled with sugar. These are
presented in a leaf platter (dauna), and then eaten
by the members of the household. An iron cuttinginstrument is also kept near the mother and child
during the period of pollution. If the child be a boy
the father is expected, to entertain his friends, which
is usually don\) on t~e twelt4 day.
On that day the\ parents or brothers of the
mother-if they can ~fford ,it-send her a coat and
cap made of red cloth fOf the baby, and a yellow
loin-cloth for the moj:,'her.;This 'present is sometimes
accompanied by a specil')l sort of sweetmeat khown
as suthaura (sonth, dxy gmger), made of sugar,
ginger, and other-spices; sometimes with the suthaura is sent some candle (achhwani). There i~
no 'distinct trace of the couvade, except that the
husband has to take the first sup of the cleaning
draught given to the mother, and that he does not
shave for six days after his wife's delivery. There
are no special ceremonies in connection with twine,
but they are considered inauspicious. If during the
pl!egnancy of a woman an eclipse happen to occur,
she is made to sit quiet while it lasts with a stone
pestle in her hand, and is not allowed to move or
touch any cutting instrument. If she move it is
believed that her child will be deformed, and if she
'ouch a cutting implement, that, it will borne
mutilated. The child is named by the senior member
of the family. On the fourth or fifth day after the
mother rejoins her family, the child's head is
shaved (munran), and when about six months old,
it is fed for the first time on grain· (annaprasan)
it is at this time that it is usually named. At the
age of five or seven its ears are bored (Kanchhedan)
and this constitute the initiation: after this the
ohild must conform to the rules of the tribe regarding food.
10. Adoption: When it is proposed to adopt a
boy, the olansmell are invited and in their presellc~

29X
the parents make over the boy to the adopter with
these words-"You were my Bon by a deed of evil
(Pap); now you are the son of so-and-so by !I.
virtuous act (dharm)." As the boy is accepted,
the members of the caste sprinkle rice over him and
the adopter gives a feast.

11. Betrothal: The customs of betrothal vary
~omewhat in different places. Thus, in Mirzapur,

when a marriage is proposed, the bridegroom's
father with his uncle and other near relations visit
the bride. She is carefully examined to make sure
that she has no physical defect, and, if approved,
the boy's father gives her a rupee, and some coarse
suagr is distributed. Then her father entertains the
party. Next follows the regular betrothal (barrekhi).
This generally takes place at the village liquor shop,
where the two fathers exchange platters (dauna)
full of liquor five times, and at the last turn the
bride's father puts a rupee into the cup of his relation-to-be. Liquor is served round, two-thirds of
the cost of which is paid by the father of the boy,
a,nd one-third by the father of the girl. On this
day the date of the wedding is fixed by the Pandit.
In BaHia, on the contrary, the parents and relations
of the girl go to the boy's house and present him
with a rupee and loin-cloth. This is known as paupuja, or "the worshipping of the feet" of the
bridegroom.
When these presents are received in the prel!lence
of the members of the caste, the engagement i.
complete.

12. Marriage Ritual: Marriage is of two kindsthe shadi, charh, charhaua, which is the respectable
form, and the dola, used by poor people. In Mirza.pur the wedding invitation is distributed by the
father's sister's husband of the boy. The marriage
pavilion (mamo) is then erected. In the Gangetie
valley it consists of four bamboos; Chamars above
the hills make it of nine poles of the siddh tree
(Hardwickia binata) in obvious imitation of the
Dravidian races by whom they are surrounded.
On this day the Pandit ties round the wrist of th&
bride an amulet formed of mango leaves and thread.
The next day is devoted to feeding the clansmen,
and cakes of various kinds are offered to the sainted
dead. Then follows the matmangara ceremony.
Then as the procession starts, the bridegroom'!!
mother does the wave ceremony (parachhan) to
keep off evil spirits. With the Bame object the bride'.
mother puts some lamp-black on the bride'e eyelidsl and hangs a necklace of beeds round her necks~

294
At the same time, as an assertion or acknowledgement of maternity, she offers the girl her breast.
The bridegroom's father is expected to take with
the procession five ankel rings (mathiya) for the
bride. The marriage is then performed by making.
the pair revolve five times round the ploughbeam
(ahris), which is fixed in the centre of the pavilion.
There also is erected a rough wooden representation
of a flock of parrots; but the pole on which they were
hung is carefully kept for a year. During the
marriage, a special dance known as the natua nach,
is performed by members of the tribe some of whom
dress in women's clothes. Chamars can give no
explanation of this practice, which may possibly
be a symbolical caremony done with the hope that
the first child may be a'boy, as the Argive brides
used to wear false beards when they slept with their
husbands.* It is specially to be noticed that
Brahmans are not employed in the marriage
ceremony. The whole business is done by the uncle
and brother-in-law (phupha, bahnoi) of the bridgegroom. Before they leave the pavilion a goat or
ram is sacrificed to Paramesari Devi, and the flesh
is cooked at the marriage feast. The marriage ends
with a general carouse at the nearest liquor shop.
13. The doW, marriage is done in quite a different
way. The following is the ritual at Ballia. The
friends and relations are invited to attend at the
bridegroom's house, and they are supplied with a
meal known as kelewa, which ordinarily cOllHi'lts
of rice and pulse or parched grain (sattus) -or wheat
cakes. The men then proceed to the bride's house
and halt about a mile off to take refreshment. The
boy's father subscribes twelve pice and the other
two pice each with which liquor is purchased. The
sum given by the boy's father is known as batsari
or Disari, and that contributed by his friends behri.
After drinking they go to the bride's hou;e, which
they reach usually about sunset. There the guardian
of the boy pays twenty-four pice, known as neg,
to the father of the bride, who supplements it with
sufficient to provide another drink for the party.
Then they are all fed, and next morning they go
away with the bride. The boy's guardian presents
two sheets (sari), one for the bride and one for
her mother, and gives a couple of rupees to her
father, who in return gives a loin cloth (dhoti)
and a sort of handkerchief worn over the shoulder
(kandhawar) to the boy, as well as a sheet for-his
.mother. The barber, washerman, and village
watchman, receive a present of two annas each on
this occasion. Sometimes the owner of the village

charges a rupee as marwachh or marwana (manro,
the muptial shed), which is paid by the father of
the btidegroom, and may perhaps be a survival
of a commutation of the jus prima nocta, but is
more probably one of the ordinary village dues
levied from tenants by the landlord. This, however,
is not. invariably taken, and in return he usually
supplies some wood, etc., for the wedding. The bride
is supplied by her guardian with a sheat (sari),
brass bracelets (mathi), and anklets (pairi), made
of bell-metal. Her brother or some other person as
her representative accompanies her to the house
of the bridegroom. It is peculiar custom that on
this occasion he always walks behind the bride.
In the dola form of marriage the bridegroom or his
father very seldom goes to the house of the bride.
The duty of escorting the bride home is left to
some relation or clansman.
14. After the bride has arrived, th,at very day
or very soon after the date of the wedding (lagan)
is fixed. The family barber takes ten pieces of turmeric, of w]lich he gives five to the bride and five
to the bridegroom. With this he brings one and-aquarter seers of paddy, which he divides equally
Hetween them. The turmeric is ground into a paste
which is rubbed on the foreheads of the pair,
and the paddy is parched and made into lawa
for use in the ceremony of lawa parachhana.
This part of the ritual is called haldi or haldidhan.
The next day or a day after comes the ceremony
matkor or "the- digging of the earth." This commences by the bridegroom's mother worshipping
a drum (dhol). If his mother be dead, this is done
1Jy his aunt or some other elderly female relation.
Turmeric and rice are ground into a paste (aipan).
'rhe woman smears her hand in this and applies
it to the drum. This is known as thappa lagana.
A leaf of betel, a betel nut, and two pice are also
placed on the drum, which are the perquisite of
the owner. Five marks (tika) are then made on the
drum with vermilion, and the women form a procession and go into a field, led by the drummer
playing away vigor{)usly.
Tlie senior oman
then worships Dharti Mata or Mother Eartb and
digs five spadesful of earth, which are br~ught home
and placed in the courtyard. In the mIddle of the

lid. Near it is a plollghbeam (haris) and a gree.n
bamboo ~ed in. tl,le e~rth. The e&rthen pot lS

16. Death Ceremonies: Those who have been
initiated into the Siva Narayani or Sri Nllrayani,
Kabirpanthi or Ramanandi sects are buried, unless
before death they have exprcssed a wish to be cremated. Their corpses are removed to the burialground on a gaily decorated bier without any marks
of mourning and accompanied with shouts of Ram;
Ram Sat hai!-"The Lord is the Lord of Truth". OrdiMry chamars are burnt in the usual way. Those
who are poor only scorch the face of the corpse
(mukhag). The ashes, when the body is properly
cremated, are thrown into some neighbouring
stream. The chief mourner, who has fired the
pyre, on the day after ,cremation places outside
house an earthen pot full of milk and rice gruel
(roanr) with a pitcher of water for the use Qf the
disembodied spirit. On the third day after death
cornes the tiratri 'ceremony which oonsiats of th~
MB/(N) lOSOH.P.-20(a)

offering of oblations and cakes of barley-flour
(pinda) to the departed soul. On the tenth day
(daswan), this ceremony is repeated, and the castemen are fed. On that day the person who fired
the pyre (dagih-a) is purified by being shaved. On
the /eleventh the utensils and private property of
the dead man are made over to his sister's husband
(bahnoi), who acts as the officiant priest-perhaps
a survival of the matriarchate. In some places,
however, and particularly where Cha'TIars are
becoming rich and influential, the Mahabrahman
offers the sacred balls (pinda). When the service
is d!}ne by a member of the tribe he says-Ar Ganga,
par Ganga; Bihari Ka beta, Rambakhsh ka Nati,
pindadet; Ganga Mai bujbuji det-"Ganges on this
side, Ganges on that side, the son of Bihari (or
whatever his name may be) the grand~on of Rambakhsh ofters the cakes, but Mother Gange~ gives
only bubbles in return". Some plant a few stalks
of grass near a tank as an abode for the spirit which
wanders about until the funeral ceremonies ar~
complete. On this water is poured daily for ten
days. Some again give a tribal feast on the twelfth,
some on the sixteenth day after death. On the
anniversary of a death twelve balls are offered,
and, if the family can afford it, the clansmen are
fed. Some, again, after the usual ball1.' and oblations during the fortnight (pitra paksha) sacred
to the dead, join in removing the corpse and each
of the five touches his mouth with a burning brand.
By this procedure none of the five incurs any personal defilement.

17. Religion : Chamars in the main conform to
the popular type of village Hinduism. To the east
of the Province all, except the richer and more
advanced members of the caste, dispense with the
services of Brahmans, except in so far as they usually
consult them about the marriage auspices. To the
west their marriage ceremonies are performed under
the guidance of the low Gurra or Chamarwa Brahmans. To the east as they become rich and influential, they employ Sarwariya or Kanaujiya Brahmans of a degraded type. To the west the mourners accompanying the corpse address the Creator
in the words-Tuhi hai, tain ne paids Riya, aur
tain ne mar liya. "Thou art He; Thou hast created
and then destroyed". In Rohilkhand their clan
deities are Bhawani, Jagiswar or "the lord of the
world", Kala Deo, Gaja Dewat, Zahir Pir, and
Nagarl'.en. In Agra they call themselves of the
Gorakhi aect, and workshop Devi, Chamara, and
Kuanwa.la, "he of the well": In Ballia they usually

296
worship a. deity whom they call Parameo Nar or "the
Supreme Being". The godling is supposed to dwell
in a mound of earth erected in a room of the house.
On the day of the Dasahra festival seven wheaten
oakes and some halwa are offered, and some cloves
and cardamoms are ground up and mixed in water,
which is poured on the ground. This is known as
ohhak. Sometimes the offering consists of a young
pig and some spirits. When a person is absent from
home, he does not erect any mound or oratory
(deokur) until he returns. In Mirzapur they have
a special deity known as Tera Deva or "the crooked
one;" they also workship the Vindhyabasini Devi,
of Bindhachal; Banru Bir, a demon of whom they
know nothing but the name; Sairi Devi, Birtiya,
and the sainted dead (purkha log). All these deities
are worshipped in times of trouble with the sacrifice of a pig, the meat of which is eaten by the
worshippers and with a libation of spirits. On the
Pachainyan festival milk and parched grain are
offered at the hole occupied by the domestic snake.
Those who have no children fast and wore hip the
sun godling, Suraj Narayan, in the hope of offspring. Fire and the moon are also occasionally
worshipped. To the east their chief festivals are
the snake feast at the Pachainyan; the Kajari,
which is a Bort of saturnalia held in the rainy season
when women drink and the rules of modesty are
held in abeyance; the Tij, on which women fast
for the welfare of their husbands and sons,
.and next day eat cakes (puri); the P-hagua or
Holi. A second wife wears an image representing
the deceased, known as sirajna, round the neck,
and when she puts on fresh clothes or jewellery she
touches them first with the image as a sign that
they have been offered to the spirit of her predecelSor. If this be not done, it is believed that the
offended spirit of the first wife will bring disease
or death.

18. The siunarayan or Srinarayani Sect. But
_he most remarkable form of worship is that of
the deistic revivalist sect of the Srinarayani or
Siunarayani. The founder of this sect was Raedas
or Ravidas, who was a disciple of Ramanand.
Curiously enough in the Dakkhin quite a different
legend has been invented and the so-called Rohidas
is said to have been born at Chambhargonda now
Ahmadnagar, and is described as a contemporary
of Kabir in the twelfth or thirteenth century. The
Northern India legend, as recorded in the commentary of Priya Das on the Bhaktmala, tells how a
Brahman disciple of Ramanand used daily to re~ive the necessary alms frOtJ1 the .1w~es C?f five

REPRINTS

Brahmans. This was cooked by hi~ preceptor, and
offered to the Creator before being eaten. One day
as it waS raining and the houses of the Brahmans
were at a distance, the Brahmachari accepted the
supplies from a Banya. When Ramanand cooked
it, the Divine Light refused to accept it, as it was
lmclean. The preceptor made enquiries and discovered that the Banya had money dealings with
Chamars and that the food was heI\ce defiled.
Ramanand, in his displeasure caused his disciple
to be reborn in the womb of a Ohamarin; and so it
happened. When the infant was born, remembering its past life it refused to suck the breast of its
mother because she was not initiated. Then a voice
from Heaven f\poke to Ramanand and warned him
that the punishment he had inflicted on his disciple
was disproportionate to his offence. He was directed
to go to the nut of the Ghamar and initiate the
whole family. He was compelled to obey this
order. The child was named by his parents Raedas.
When he reached the age of 18 he begaiMo worship
a clay image of Rama and J anaki. ThiS waS displeasing to his father, wh9 turned him out of doors.
Raedas then set up business as a shoemaker and
continued his mode of worship. He used to present
all wandering ascetics with new shoes. One day a
sain~ appeared before him and gave him the philosopher's stone. Raedas took no notice of it; but the
Saint touched his shoemaker's knife with it and
turned it into gold. This had no effect on Raedas,
and the saint finally left the stone in the thatch of
his hut. Returning some time after he found Rasdas in poor circumstances, and learned to his surprise that he had not us~d the stone. The saint
then promised that before morning 5 gold coin~
would appear in front of the divine image whioh
Raedas worshipped. These he also refused to accept.
But he was warned in a drea,m not to continue to
despise wealth; so he converted his shed into a.
magnificent temple and established regular worship.
This enraged the Brahmans, who appealed to the
Raja in a Sanskrit verse which means. "Where
unholly thingi are worshipped and holy things are
·defiled, three things follow-FaI~ine, death, and
Fear".
19. Raedas was summdned before the Raja and
ordered to exhibit his miraculous powers. He
replied that he could do only one miracle-that the
Salagrama or ammonite representing Vi~hnu would
at his word leave its place and come down on the
palm of his hand. The Raja ordered the Brahmans
to perform a similar miracle. They failed and
Rae,das succeeded. ~J:Ps miracle .so ~ffected ,the

Rani Jhali, whom one version of the legend makes

became initiated. On this the Brahmans refused
to eat in the palace, on the ground that it had been
defiled, and some raw grain was given to them which
they began to cook in the garden. But as they
we~e eating they suddenly saw Raedas sitting and
eatmg between two Brahmans. So they fell at his
feet, and then he cut his skin and showed them
llIlder it his Brahmanical cord; so he was proved
to have heen 8, Brahman in his former life.

wrote It about 17~5 A.D. The most important o'f
these' workS! are -vhe Gun\nyasa and the Santa

and gives an accoUl).t of tile ten Avataras of Vishnu
or N!trayana in fJ:.lUrte~n chapters, of which the
first six treat of the aut~or, of faith, of the punishment of sinners, of virtue, 6f a future state and of
