21. Siunarayanis have a meeting house known
as Dhamghar, or "House of Paradise"; SOlllaghar,
or "House of meeting", and Girja Ghar or church,
a word derived through the Portuguese igreja from
the Greek ekklesia. It usually contains- pictures
of the SaintB Gorakhnath, Raedas, Kabirdas,
Surdas and others. The scriptures are kept rolled
up in cloths on a table at the east. They are carefully
watched and never given to anyone but members
of their own congregation. They meet here Oll Friday
evenings, and any educated man among them reads
and expolmds passages from the Gurunyasa. The
only occasion when the Santavirasa is read is at
death; it is then recited from the moment of dissolution until the corpse is buried. They are not
allowed to eat meat or drink spirits before goin a to
ihe weekly service, but this is the only restriction.
On the Basant Panchami, or fifty light half of
Magh, a Halwai is called in, who cooks some halwa
sweetmeat (which is known as manbhog, or "food
of the mind") in a large boiler (karhao). This is
first offered to Siunarayana before the Scriptures
of the sect, and until this is done no Chamar is
allowed to tou~h it. The explanation of this is that
Siunarayana was a Chhatri, and it would be de£le-

ment to him if any Chamar touched it before dedicgtion. An offering of the same kind is made to Guru
Nanak by the Sikhs.
22. Admit outsiders: The title Bhagat which they
take does not imply that they abstain from flesh
and spirits, but they are monotheists (Sanskrit
bhakta "devoted"). They say that their chief
conventicle is at a place called Barsari, in the Ghazipur District, about which they repeat the verse,"As pas Chandrawar men Ghazipur Sarkar Bindu
nirauni karat sab Bagh rae kepas'!. In the neighbourhood of Chandrawar in the Ghazipur District,
all meet together and discuss the doctrine of Unity.
This place is near the Rae's garden".

23. Persons of any caste may join the Siunarayani sect. When a candidate wishes to affilia.te
himself, they first warn him of the difficulties before
binI and test him for a few days, When, if approved.
he,is direc~ed to bring a present according to his
means to the headman, known as Guru or Mahant.
The candidate comes before the Guru, who sits
with the scriptures opposite him, and first make!
a sacrifice by burning camphor q,nd dason, or ten
kinds of perfumes. These are thrown on fire, and
the sweet savours which arises is their form of
worship. Then some camphor is burnt before the
scriptures, and all present rub the smoke over their
faces. The candidate then washes the big toe of the
Guru and drinks the water (charanamrita). Next
the Guru recites privately into his ear the
formula (mantra) of initiation which is carefully
concealed from outeiders. After this the initiate
distributes sweets to the congregation. He is then
considered Sant or initiate, and receives a small
book which he is permitted to study, and which
serves as a pass of admission to furture meetings.
If he loses it he has to appear at the next Basant
Panchami meeting, and pay two and-a-falf rupees
for a new copy, as well as a fine of five rupees for
his negligence. At these meetings there is musio
and singing, men and women sit apart, and after
the Mahant has finished his reading, he receives
the contributions of the faithful. They are not
allowed to drink in the Dhamghar, but they may
smoke ganja, bhang, or tobacco there. They never
practise exorcisms (ojhai), nor do they get into a
state of religious frenzy and deliver oracles. As
already stated the dead are buried with signs of
rejoicing. Some camphor is burnt in the grave
before the body i~ lai.d there, and then all present
join in filling up the grave. All initiates, male and
female, buried in this way. Children and person

298
not initiated are interred without any ceremony.
If the wife of an initiate die, her relatives can take
away her body and cremate it. They marry like
Qrdinary Chamars, and get a· Brahman to fix a lucky
time. A similar movement among the Chamars
of Bilaspur in th~Central Provinces took place under
Ghasidas between ~820 and 1830, and in Bikaner
under Lalgir about fifty years ago. Their sole
worship is said to consist in calling on the invisible
lord (Alakh, Alakh).
24:. Demonology The ordinary Chamar believes
that dhease, death, and all troubles are due to
demoniacal influence. When a.person falls ill a
sorcerer (Ojah) is called in and he points out tho
particular evil spirit which is responsible for the
mischief and the approprif>te ~acrifice by means of
which he can be appeased. In the same way
barrenness in women is held to be due to her possesion by some demon. A widow is very careful to
worship the spirit of her deceased husband. In
this case as with a deceased wife no image is used,
but a piece of ground is plastered, and on it is
placed a new loin-cloth (dhoti), and a waist chain
(kardhani). Sometimes a pig is sacrificed. The
soul of a dead husband is called manushya deva
or "the mangod". Persons who die in any sudden
or unusual way become malevolent spirits (bhut),
and must be carefully propitiated. Their offering
is a young pig and an oblation of spirits. Chickens
are offered to Ghazi-Miyan, goats to Devi, and pigs
to the family godlings and evil spirits. These are
offered at the house shrine, while offerings to
godlings and saints are made at their temples or
tombs. The regular feast in honour of the dead
is the Mahalaya Amawas Pitrbisarjan or Pitrasaunan. Among trees they respect the pipal, tulasi,
and nim. The pipal is the abode of Vasudeva,
the tulasi of Lakshmi, the nim of Sitala. Mother
,Ganges (Ganga mai) is a special object of reverence.
The favourite method of propitiating evil spirits
of those who have died by accident is to pour
spirits near the place occupied by the Bhut, and to
light some ganja in a pipe bowl. For ghosts cf
high caste persons, the proper offering is a fire
sacrifice (hom). The ordinary malignant evil
spirit is called Bhut or Daitya; that of a Muhammadari Shahid Mard; the Jinn is higher and more
powerful than these. To the Shahid, Mard and
Jinn the sacrifice is not a pig but a fowl and flowers.
25. Social Regulations: The Chamar from his
occupation and origin ranks even below the nOll~ryan ~ribe.s who hav~ been Guite recently adopted
mto Hmdmsm. He IS considered impure becaues

he eats beaf, pork, and fowls, all abomination
to the orthoaox Hindu. He will eat cattle which
die a natural death, and numerous cases have
oocurred where Chamars havo poisoned cattle for
the sake of the hides and flesh. He keeps herds of
pigs and the Chamr!1uti or Chamar quarter in a
Hindu village is generally a synonym for a place
abounding in all kind of abominable filth, where a
olean living Hindu seldom, unless for urgent
necessity, cares to intrude. One proverb describes a
man setting up to be Gopal, a respectable Krishna
worshipper, while his pots and pans are as filthy as
those of a Chamar (Nem tern Gopal aisan; hami
charui Chamar aisan), and another says,-"The
worthy are dying and the unworthy living because
Chamars are drinking Ganges water,"-(Lajulu
mare, dhithau jiyo; Ganga jal Chamaran piye).
This repugnance to him is inoreased by his eating
the leavings of a4nost any caste except Dhobis
and Doms, and by the pollution which \attaches
to his wife (Chamarin, chamain), who 'acts as
midwife and cuts the umbilical cord. But in spite
of his degraded social position, the Cham(1r is
proud and punctilious and very conservative as
regards the rights and privileges which he receives
in the, village community. Their women 'wear,
at leadt in the east of the Province, no nose rings;
they have metal bangles (mathaya) on their wristw,
arm ornameD-ts (baju) and heavy bell-metal anklets
(pairi). Chamars swear by Rama, the Guru,
the Ganges, Maha deva Baba, the shoemaker's
last (pharuhi), and their sons heads. They will
not touch a Dom or Dhobi, nor the wife of a.
younger brother or nephew, nor will they call
their wives by their names. Women eat after the
men. I They salute relatives and clansmen in the
forms Ram! Ram! and paelagi.
26. Occupations: The Chamar practises a VAriety
of occupations. His primary business is curing
skins and shoemaking, and the latter business has
developed what is really a 1)eparate caste, that of
the Mochi (Sans. Mochlka); ~n'a village he provides
all leathern articles used in husbandry, such as
whips, thpngs, well bucketl'!, and the like. As a
rule, he has a circle of constituents (jaj~an) whose
dead cattle he receives, and to whom he gives
leather and a certain number of shoes in return.
His wife has similarly a~ certain number of families
to 'whom she acts as midwife and performs various
menial services at marriages and festivals. The
Chamar himself is the~general village drudge (begarJ
pharait) runa messages, and doei odd jobs, 8 ,(

as thatching when he is called Gharami, and the
like. Sometimes he receives wages in cash or kind
but pcrhaps more generally an allowance of grain
per plough belonging to the family he serves, or a
patch of rcnt-fre~ land. Another part of his duties,
is to beat drums and blow trumpets during a
marriage or when cholera or other epidemic disease
is being exercised from the village. Large numbers
of Chamars tak~tofield labour, act as ploughmen,
carters, grooms, or emigrate to towns, where they
do various kinds of un~killed work. In Partabgarh
they are said to have usurped the business of
carrying palanquin~, the hereditary occupation of
Kahars. The extension of the leather trade at
Cawnpore has ma~ it a grf'at Chamar centre.
Many of them have ~ecome wealthy and aim at a
standard of social respectability 'much higher than
thcir rural bretheren,l and some have begun even

as soon as he 60mmehces ~o rise in the world.

27. The system of t~nninlpursued by the, ordinary village Chamar i~ of t~e most primitive kin_~.
The skins are placed in a pit I and covered with
water, containing lime (chuna) and impure carbonate of soda (sajji); after tel;l. days they are taken
out land the hair removed with an iron scraper
(khurpi). They are again repl.oved, sewn up in
the form of a bag, which is ,again filled with the
bark solution, and hung on a tree or stand. This
process lasts five days, when the tanning is
considered complete.

The Ohamar·: The Chamar is the tanner and
leather-worker of North-Western India, and in the
western parts of the Panjab he is called Mochi
whenever he is, as he generally is, a Musalman, the
caste being one and the same. The name Ohamar
is derived from the Sanskrit Oharmakara or
"worker in hides." But in the'east of the Province

he is far more than a ieather-worker. He is the
general coolie and field labourer of the villages; and
a Chamar, if asked his caste by an Englishman a.t
any rate, will answer "Coolie" as often as "Chamar"l.
They do all the begar, or such work as cutting
grass, carrying wood and bundles, acting a8 watchme.n, and the like; and they plaster the houses with
mud when they need it. They take the hides of
all dead cattle and the flesh of all colven-footed
animals, that of such as do not divide tht.' hoof
going to Chuhras. They make and mend shoes,
thongs for the cart, and whips and other leather
work; and above all tbey do an immense deal of
hard work in the fields, each family supplying
eaoh cultivating assooiation with the oontinuous
labour of a certain number of hands. All this
they do as village menials, receiving fixed customary dues in the shape of a shart -of the produce of
the fields. In the e!:\st lind. south-east of the
Panjab the village Chamars also do ~ great deal
of weaving, which howe.ver is paid for'{leparately.
The Chamars stand far above the Chuhras in
Bocial position, and some of their tribes are almost
accepted as Hindus. Their religon is sk{'~ohed in
section 294. They are generally dark in colour,
and are almost certainly of aboriginal origin,
thqugh here again their numbers have perhaps
been swollen by members of other and higher castes
who have fallen or been degraded. The people
say: ('Do not cross the "ferry with a black
Brahman or a fair Chamar," one being as unusual
as the other. Their Women are celebrated for
beauty, and loss of caste is often attributed to too
great partiality for a Chamarni. Sherring has a
long disquisition on the Chamar caste, whieh
appears to be much more ext,ensive and tQ include
much more varied tribes in Hindustan than in the
Panjab.

The Sikh Cham~r or Ramdasia-It will be seen
:from Table VIII-A that in the north and 'Centre
of the Eastern Plain.s a very considerable number
of Chamars have embraced the Sikh religion. These
,men are .called Ramdasia after Guru Ram Das,
tflough what connection ,they have with him I have
been unable to disdover. Perhaps he was the first
Guru to admit Chamars to the religion. Many,
perhaps most of the Ramdasi Chamars have abandoned leather-work for loom, they do not eat carrion, and they occupy a much higher position
than the Hindu Chamars, though they are not
admitted to religious equality by the other Sikhs.
The Ramdasi are often confused with the Raidasi
or Rabdasi Chamars. The former are true Sikhs
and take the pahul. The latter are Hindus, or if
Sikhs, only Nanakpanthi Sikhs and do not take the
pahul; and are followers of Bhagat Rav Das or Rah
Das, himself a Chamar; They are apparently as
true Hindus as any Chamars oan be, and are wrongly called Sikhs by confusion with the Ramdasias
(See further, paragraph 608). The Musalman Chamar or Moohi (Caste No. 19)-The word Mochi is
properly the name of an ocoupation, and signifies
the worker in tanned leather as distinguished from
the tanner. The Mochi not o.nly makes leather lutioles, but he alone grains leather and gives it a
surface Co.lour or stain, as distinguished form a.
oolour dyed throughout. In the east'of the Panjab
the name is usually applied only to the more skilled
workmen of the towns. In the west howeyer, it is
1

simply used to designate a Musalman Chamar;
and the Mochi there is what the Chamar is in the
east and belongs to. the same caste, though his
change of religion improves, though only slightly
his social position. He does not ordinarily weave,
though in Hushyarpur the majority of the Mochis
are said to be weavers, and he is not admitted
to religious or social communion by the other
Musalmans. In the west of the Panjab, however,
the Chamar or Mochi no. longer occupies that important position as an agrioulturallabourer that he
does in the east. In the west he is merely a tanner
and leather-worker, and his numbers are proportionally less than when a large part of the field
work is done by him. Moreover, he no longer renders
menial service; and it may be that his improved
social positioIl is partly due to this fact. Mr. Christie
indeed, says that so soon as a Chamar, whether
Hindu or Musalman, abandons menial offices and
oonfines himself to working in leather, he rises in
the social scale and assumes the more respeotable
name of Mochi, The Moohi is proverbially un·
punctual in rendering service, and there is a saying,
"The Moohi's to-morrow never oomes." To the
figures for Moohis must he added those who. are
shown in Abstract No. 72 (page 224) as having
returned themselves as Jats.

Divisions of Ohamars and Mochis : The tribes of
the Chama! caste are innumerable, and some of
them vary large. It does not seem worth while to
give any tabular statement, as to include anything
like even half the total number a very long list

80 I am told Mr. Wilson, however, say. that he hall neTer hell.rd the word used.

302
would have to be shown. But it is worth while
eomparing the figures for Chamars and Mo chis
for a few of the largest tribes. This is done in the
bargin. Only the first seven tribes are found in
!ny numbers among the Chamars of the Delhi
and Hissar divisions. Nos. 4 and 7 are the principal
ones of the Ambala division; while these two last,
together with Nos. 8 to 18, are found in the
Jalandhar division. Among the Mochis the Bhatti
and Chauhan tribes are the most numerous.
It, is obvious that many of these tribal names are
merely taken from the dQminant race in whose
service tho tribe was formed.
Ramdasia is of
course a ,religious and. not a tribal division; and
doubtless many of the sub-divisions returned are
merely clans, and included in the larger tribes.
This last point will be shown in the detailed tables.
But it appears that the Chamars of the Eastern
Panjab may be broadly divided into five great
sections, the Jatia, the Raidasi, the Chamar, the
Chandar, and the Golia or Raigar, no one of which
intermarries with the others. The J atia are found
in gr.eatest numbers about the neighbourhood of
DelhI and Gurgaon. They work in horse and camel
hides, whioh are a_n abomination to the Chandar
probably as having the foot uncloven; and are perhaps named from the word Jat (hardt) a camel
grazier. On the other hand, they are said to obtain
the services of Gaur Brahmans, whioh would
put them above all other Chamars, who have to be
content with the ministrations of the outoast
Cbamarwa Brahman. The Raidasi or Rabdasi Chamars are Darned after rai Das Bhagat, himself a
Chamar, a contemporary of Kabir and like him a
di~cip:e of Ramanand. They are the prevalent
~rlbe III Kamal and the neigh bOUThood. Th6 Golia
IS the lowest of all the sections; and indeed the
wor~ Golia is ~he name of a section of many of the
memal castes ill the Eastern Panjab and in almost
all cases carries with it an inferior standing in the
caste. The Cbamar comes between the J atia and
the Golia, a:iJJ is the prevalent tribe further west,
ab0l!t J alandhar and Ludhiana. The Chandar is
the highest of all, and is said in Delhi to trace its
origin from Banaras, probably from some association with1{abir. It is the principal section in Hi~sar
and Sirsa. They, do not tan, leaving that to the
Chamarangs and Khatiks, and working only in
ready preparcrlleather. There are doubtless similar
tribal distindions among the Chamars of tho central
districts; but I have no information regarding
them.

Chanal-Or probably Channal, from Chanaala,
whom all Sanskrit authorities represent as begotten
by a Sudra on a Brahman. His occupation is
carrying out corpses executing criminal, and
other abject offices for the public service. * The
menial-class of Kangra and Mandi,corresponding
to the Dagi in Kullu and the Koli in the
Simla hills, the chanals in Kangra appe~ to be
inferior to- the Kolis of that District, and ~me of
them at least will not touch dead cattle, or mix on
equal terms with tliose who do. On the other }land
in KulIu Saraj some of the chanals rank below Kalis.
Dagi-Chanal is a very common term for the caste:
and in KuHu it appears to include the Nar. Yet
a Chan'al of Mandi State will not intermarry with a
Dagi of Kullu. The Chanal is also found in Chamba,
where the proverb goes: Chanal, Jetha, Rathi
Kanetha, 'The low caste is the elder and the Rathi
the younger brother, doubtless pointing to a t~adi.
tion that the chanal represents and earlier of
aboriginal race. See the articles on Dagi and Roli.

The Daoli: Under this head I have included
87 men who have returned themselves as Sansoi,
as this appears to be the usual name in the higher
ranges for the Daoli of the low hills: The Daolis are
men who wash gold from the beds of mountain
streams, and are naturally found only in the hills,
those returned for Patiala be5llg inhabitants of the
hill territory which belongs to that State. They
also work the water-mills which are so common on
the mountain torrents. Most of them are Hindus
a few Sikh, and none-Musalman. These men are
outcastes of about the same status as the Dumpa;
indeed they are said by many to belong ~o the
Dumna caste, and it appears that they also make
matting and the like.

Turi: (1) The mus\cian of the Simla Hills who
beats the drum when /a corpse is carried out to the
burning ground. They get a share in the offerings
of thp dead and receive the shroud, 'besides getting
fees in proportion to the means of the deceased~
Th~y are also given grain at harvest time. As they
take tlle offerings of the dead they are considered
unclean, and rank a little lower than a B~ri or
Lohar in the hills. They also generally do sewing
work also. 'fhey marry in their own community,
and in the upper hills a Kanet may not cohabit
with a Turi woman, but in the lower he could not
be outcasted for so doing. A Rahu or Kuran Kanet
might possibly keep a Turi woman without much
risk of excommunication. The Turi generally
are not agriculturists, but live by singh'1g and
musical performances.

(2) Early in the 16th century some 60 families.
of a Hindki or Indian race called Turi moved up
from their earlier seats opposite Nilii.b on the Indus
in the Attock district into the Kurram valley, where
they settled down as hamsayas Or vassals of the
Bangash Pathans or 'Pathans of the Bangashat."
Weakened by internal dissensions and migration
to Kohat, the Bangash lost ground, while the Turis
were reinforced by fresh arrivals from home, who
were attracted by the fertility of the Kuram
valleys, and gradually supplanted the Bangash in
lts possession until only two villages, Shalozan*

and Zeran, remained to the latter. The Turia
howeve~ had adopted the Shia tenets of their
overlordb and still retain them. Each family has
it.s hereditlA-.:y mourners, who possess great influence
and take thl' place of the mullahs in the Pathan
tribes-there being no mullahs among the Turis,
who as Shias pay great reverence to Sayads-and
the matim kotha or mourning house of the village,
at which on every Friday and 13 days in the Moharram all the villagers assemble, is the centre of their
religious life. They must make, if means permit, a
pilgrimage to Karbala and Mashhad, and arc closely
oonnected with Persia.
On the other hand, few go to Macoa. t
The Turis are also called Panjplara or the fin
fathers and are divided into as many sections O!:
clans whose descent, real or fictitious, is thus givE'n I

Of the cultivated area of the valley the Turis now
hold 37 per cent., which is distributed as above.
The Landizai and Duperzai evidently took shares
according to the rule of chundavand, i.e., the groups
of sons of each mother took an equal share. Eaoh
section is further subdivided into septs or families
and each had its sarishta khel or chief family in
which was vested authority to collect levies for
war and make other executive arrangements. When
on its occupation by the Turis the valley was divided among the five clans to each was allotted a
-palarina or ancestral share in the country, and
these are still remembereG.. Within each clan's
plarina thE' Turis constituted themselves naiks or
overlords in their turn and all the races under them
were regarded as their hamsayas or dependents.
There were four such groups. First, the conquerred
Bangash and other tribes who were settled on the
mountain slopes for purposes of defence. These had
to pay certain dues in token of allegiance and were
further bound not to sell or mortgage their lands

*Sha,)ozan Village is said to be closely connected with the provinces ofMazenderan and Azirbaij an. It is noted for the beauty of
its women.
tRaverty regarded the Turis as Roshanias, not as Shias. They have or had a curious custom. "Then they meet a stranger, they ask
first ifhe is 'straight' Or 'crooked', putting the forefinger to the forehead, first. straight, then bent. By 'straight' they mean Shia.

304
to anybody save their naiks. Next oame the sooial
bamsayas of the meroantile dass who paid oertain
dues (kaba) on births, betrothals, weddings, etc.,
to their overlords. Then came the pastoral, like ~he
Ghilzai nomads, who paid aues in kind for grazing,
repaired homesteads and manured fields. Lastly
came the common or agricultural hamsayas who
were virtually tenants paying produce rents. Of
all their feudal rights the only one that survives
in the case of the first or political hamsayas in the
light of pre-emption, but the obligations of the
other three classes still subsist. The old Pathan
custom of vesh or periodical re-distribution of the
'land still survives in the case of the backward
\'illage of Jallandhar and there land is allotted in
equal shares to every man, wom{tn and child every
four years. But some villages have accepted permanent partitions, while others have agreed to
divide the land till the next Revenue Settlement is
made. Again, as the valley was not conquerred at a
blow the acquisitions, had to be divided as they
were made among the various clam. The result is
that the possessions of each clan are scatterad about
over the whole valley and do not lie in compaot
blocks. But to this day a distinction is observed
cetween pioneer families who took part in the
f,arliest conquests and those who came after them.
No Turi is oonsidered as asil or of blue blood unless
he can point out his possessions in the first Turi
coloni~ of Malana and Peiwar. But some of the
clans, mostly Sara galla and a few Duperzai, are still
nomad and pastoral, and those who live in tents
and summer in Upper Kurram, but move down
in winter to the pastures of Lower Kurram are
called kuchi as opposed to those who have settled
in hamlets and are called Kothi. Intensely democratic the Turis own no chiefs and their sense of individuality is so strong that each hamlet, with its
central tower and circle of plane trees, is known by
the Iiame of its present holder and it is an offence
to call it by its founder's_ name.
The aims in life of every Turi are to marr~ the
woman he loves, murder the enemy he hates, play
the swell as a malik, collect money by corruption
and wash away his sins by a pilgrimage to Karbala.
In the famous battle at that place some Byzantine
Christians fell on the side of the sons of Ali and
that event has givan a religious touch to the Turis'
devotion to the British Government. Unfortunately

REPRINTS

the Turi though shrewd in busine.! and above
the average in intelligence is lavish in his expenditure at weddings and in bribing the jirgas. The costs
of shadi (marriage), trade (bribery) and badi (feuds)
account for about four-fifths of the alienations of
their land, but their expropriation has not yet reached alarming dimensions. Formerly splendid horsemen and born moss-troopers the Turis still make
excellent irregular soldiers, but they are litigious
and saturated with party feeling which makes them
utterly untruthful. Still their hospitality is great
and the fidelity of a Turi escort or badragga proverbial. Th9ugh darker in complexion than the
Bangash they are a fine people physically and the
kuIach or fathom* of a full-grown Turi is by repute 6l feet.

Dumna : The Dumna, called also Domra, and
even Dum in Ohamba, is the Ohuhra of the hills
proper, and is also found in large numbers in the
sub-montane traots of Kangra, Hoshiarpur and
Gurdaspur. Like the Ohuhra1of the plains he is
something more than a scavenger; but whereas the
Ohuhra works chiefly in grass, the Dumna adq.s
to this occupation the trade of workin~ in bamboo\
a material not avaqable to the Ohuhr~. He mak~
sieves, winnowing llans, fans, matting, grass ~ope
and string, and getJ-erallYt all the vessels, baskets,
screens, furniture rnd (l)$.er articles which are
ordinarily made of. bam'bo01 When the oonfines
himself to this sort of work and gives up scavengering, he appears to, 'be ~alled Bhanjra, at any
rate in the lower hills, and occassionally sarial.
The Dumna appears ,bardly ~ver to become Musalman or Sikh, and is classed as Hindu, though being
an outcast he is not allowed to draw water from
wells used by the ordinary Hindu population.

The Dumna is often called Dum in other parts
of India, as in Ohamba; and is regarded by Hindus
as the type of uncleanness. Yet he seems ~nce to
have enjoyed as a separate aboriginal race some
power and importance. Further information regarding him will be found in Sherring (1,400) and
Elliott (1,84). He is, Sir Denzil Ibbetson considered,
quite distinct from the Dum Mirasi. (250)
Dumrw-: A low sweeper caste, also called Bhanjra,

Hoshiarpur. They make Ohik5, Baskets, etc., of
bamboo and to menial service. Apparently the
term i~ a generio one, including Barwalas, Batwals,

The Dumna : The Dumna, called also Domra
and even Dum in Ohamba, is the Ohuhra of the hills
proper, and is also found in large numbers in the
sub-montane districts of Hoshiarpur and Gurdaspur.
Like the Chuhra of the plains he is something more
than a scavenger; but whereas the Ohuhra works
chiefly in grass, the Dumna adds to this occupation
the trade of working in bamboo, a material not
available to the Ohuhra. He makes sieves, winnowing pans, fans, matting grass rope and string,
and generally all the vessels, baskets, screens,
furniture, and other articles which are ordinarily
made of bamboo. When he confines himself to
this' sort of work and gives up scavengering, he
appears to be called Bhanjra, at any rate in the
lower hills, and occasionally Sarial; and 1
have included 261 Bhanjras and 31 Sarials in
my ..figures. In the Jalandhar division Bhanjras
were not returned separately from Dumnas. The
Dumna appears hardly ever to become Musalman
or Sikh, and is classed as Hindu, though being an
outcast he is not allowed to draw water from wells
used by the ordinary Hindu population.

The Dumna is often called Dum in other part <t
of India, as in Ohamba; and is regarded by Hindus
as the type of uncleanness. Yet he seems once to
have enjoyed as a separate aboriginal race some
power and importance. Further information regarding him will be found in Sherring (1,400) and
Elliott (1,84). He is of course quite distinct from
the Dum-Mirasi whom I have classed as Mirasi.
Extract from "The Punjab and its Fe'l.ldatorie;
Oensus Report, Part 1, 1891" By E. D. Maclagan.
(P.299)

The Hesi: The Hesi appears to share with the
Lohar tht' distinction of beirtg the only castes recogruzed among the Buddhists of Spiti, the other
classes of that society eating together and intermarrying freely, but excluding these two from social
intercourse. The Hesi of Spiti however, or rather
the Bedas as they are there called, the two names
referring to the same people, do not appear to have
returned themselves as of that caste, as all our
Hesis, are Hindus, whereas all the people of Spiti
except two are returned at Buddhists.

The Hesis are the wandering minstrels of the
higher Himalayan valleys. "The men play the
pipes and kettle-drum, while the women dance
and sing and play the tembourine. They are" (in
Lahul and Spiti) the only class that owns no land.
The Beda no land and the dog no load is a
proverbial saying". He is called the 18th caste,
or the odd caste which is _not required, for no one
will eat from the hands of a HesL Yet he had his
inferiors, for he himself will not eat from the hands
of a Lohar or of a Nath, the Rulu equivalent for
Jogi. He is ordinarily a begger, but sometimes
engages in petty trade; and to call a transaction "a.
Hesi's bargain" is to imply that it is mean and
paltry. Tlie Hesi or Hensi, as the word is sometimes
spelt, is found in Kangra, Mandi and SUket. To
the figures of Table VIII A should be added 201
Hesis in Suket who were left out by mistake, and
are shown under Caste No. 208, Table VIII B.

The Halis are, or claim to be, endogamous, and
would Dot at any rate give a daughter to a Badi

man may espouse his wife's sister. S~x:ual

done by her mother-in-law and is called suhagan

A divorce can remarry, but a widow may not
espouse her husband's elder brother. Widow
remarriage is celebrated by the women's putting a
dori on the bride, and her husband's placing 8
bals in her nOSe.

The J ogi, Rawal, and N ath : The figures under
the _E,ead Jogi include two very distinct classes of
peraons. Firat are the Jogis proper, a regular religious order of Hindus, which includes both the
Augar Jogis and the Kanphatta .Jogi ascetics,
who are followers of Goraklinath and priests
and worshippers of Siva. These men are fully as
respectable as the Bairagis, Gosains, and other
religious orders. So far as the sub-divisional tables
help us, the present figures include 9,143 of this
class of whom 5,769 are males, but the real number:
is probably greater. They are all Hindus. 'fhey
have been discussed in the earlier portion of this:
section, at. page 286. The second class is that
miscellaneous assortment of low-caste faqirs and
fortune-tellers, both Hindu. and Musalman but.
chiefly Musalman, who are commonly known as
J ogis. The word J ogi or Yogi means a student of
the .Toga school of philosophy, which teaches how.
by suppression of the breath, mental abstraction,
and the like, to obtain supernatural powers of
divination, second sight, and so fortht and the
result is that every rascally beggar who pretends
to be able to tell fortunes, or to prac~ise astrological
and necromantic arts in however Rmall a degree,
buys himself a drum and calls himself and is called
by others a J ogi. These men include all the
Musalmans, and probably a part of the Hindus of
the eastern districts who have been returned as
.Jogis. They are a thoroughly vagabond set, and
wander about the country beating a drum ~nd
begging, practising surgery and physic in a small
way, writing charms, telling fortunes, and practising exorcism and divination; or, settling in the

The Rawals of the Panjab are notorious cheats.
One of their favourite devices, is to personate a.
long lost relative. In the Province itself they
seldom venture upon open crime; but they travel
about the Oentral Provinces and the Deccan and
even visit Bombay and Oalcutta, and there pilfer
snd rob. They are often absent for long periods
on these expeditions; and meanwhile the Banyas
of their villages support their families on credit,
to be repaid with interest on the return of the
father. Some interesting information regarding
them will 'be found in Selected Papers, No. ~VIII
of 1869 of. the Panjab Police Department. The
town of Rawalpindi i8 named after the .Rawals;
but the Rawals of the District dppear to have
returned themselve,> either as J ogis or are probably
as Mughals, as 1,263 of the Ml!:ghals of Rawalpindi
give Rawal as their clan. There they are said, in

The Naths of the higher hins, where the worship
of Siva is prevalent, correspond very closely with
the J ogis of the plains, though they make little
pretenge to an a&cetic character and live chiefly by
growing vegetables; but they also perform certain
!lemi-sacerdotal functions, taking the place of the
Acharj of the plains in the funeral ceremonies of
the Kanets, and receiving l~ke him the clothes of
the deceased. They also consecrate new houses,
and purify them when they have been defiled.
They now form a true caste, and are not recruited
from without. One or more in almost every Nath
household has his ears pierced in honour of Siva,
and is called a Kanphatta Nath. They occupy
much the same social position as the J ogi-Ra wal of
the plains. They are understood po have returned
themselves as J ogis and to be included in the figures

such, invested with powers of control over the
material universe. The history of the development
of the modern J ogi out of the ancient professors of
Yoga is as fascinating as it is obscure, but it would
be entirely beyond the scope of this article, the
object of which is to give a matter-of-fact account
of the actual beliefs and customs of the latter-day
Jogi.
The term of Jogi may be said to include two
rery distinct classes of persons. First are the
rogis proper, a regular religious order of. Hindus,
which includes both the Aughar Jogis and the
Kanphatta J ogi ascetics who are followers of
Gorakhnath and priest and worshippers of Shiva*.
These men are fully as respectabltj as the Bairagis,
Gosains, and other religious orders. They are all
Hindus, but the gbari8hti or secular Jogi, even if
a Hindu, appears to be commonly called RAW AL
and makes a living
begging, telling fortunes,

Another synonym {oJ the. Ilindu Jogis is Nath.
The second class is thai mi&<;l~llaneous assortmen,t

complOnly kno"o/n as J ogis. "Eve;y rascally beggar
who 'pretends to be abl,e to tell fortunes, or to

calls himself, and is called by others, a J ogi. These

style themselves Jogis. They are a thoroughly
vagabond set, and wander about the country
beating a drum and begging, practising surgery
and physic in a small way, writing charms, telling
fortunes, and practising exorcism and divination;
or, settling in the villages, eke out their earnings
from these occupations by the offerings made at the

Sayads and other Musalman saints; for the .Jogi
is so impure that he will eat the offerings made at
any shrine. These people, or at least the Musalman section of them are called in the centre
of the Punjab Rawals, or sometime Jogi-Rawals,
from the Arabic'rammal, a divinder, which again
is derived from rama] , "sand", with which the

Arab magicians divine~. The Jogi-R~wal~. of
Kathiawar are said to be exorcisers of eVIl SPIrIts,

the J ogis pretend to avert storms from the npelllng
crops by plunging a drawn sword into the field or
a knife into a mound, sacrificing goats, and accepting, suitable offerings. Mr. Benton wrote:"The Jogi is a favourite character in Hindustani
fiction. He there appears as a jolly playful character of a simple disposition, who enjoys the fullest
liberty and conducts himself in the most eccentric
fashion under the cloak of religion without being
called in question." The Jogis used to be at
deadly feud with the Saniasis and 500 of the former
were once defeated by two or three hundred Saniasis.
Akbar witnessed the fight and sent soldiers
, smeared with ashes to assist the Saniasis who at
length defeated the Jogis.
The jogis as a body cannot be said to have any
history so numerous and indeterminate are tho
branches into which they have split up in the course
of time. Regarding their origius the J 9giS have a
vast body of nebulous tradition, the debris of
much primitive metaphysical speculation now
hardly recognisable in its fantastic garb.

A tradition says that Narinjan Nirankar, the
formless Creator, created Gorakh Nath from the
sweat of his breast, whence he is also called Ghor
Nath (fr. ghor, filth). The Supreme then bade
him oreate the universe, whereupon a creeping
plant sprang from'his navel, and a lotus blossomed
on it. From this flower sprang Vishnu, Brahma,
Shiva and Shakti, the 'last a woman who straightway dived beneath the waters, before earth or
sky, air or fire had been oreated. As earth was,
indispensable to the oomplete manifestation of
the universe, the Supreme sent Vishnu down to
the lower regions beneath the waters to bring
Earth to the surface. When he reached the Patsl
Lok Vishnu saw Shakti with a dhuni in front of
her, whlle light rayed from her body. A Voice asked
who had oome, and Vishnu replied that his errand
was to bring up Earth by the Supreme's command.
The Shakti answered that he could do so, provided
he first wed her, but Vishnu urged that intercourse
with her was impossible, since even at a distance
of 12 kos he found her effulgence insupportable.
So he returned unsuccessful. Brahma likewise
failed, and so at last Shivs wa~ sent. To his reply
that 'Shiva had OOIDe', the Voice said: 'There have
been crates of Shivas, which Shiva art thou 1
Shiva answered that he was the Lord of Kailas,

and he agreed to espouse Shakti when Earth and
sky had come into being. Shakti then gave forth
the four Vedas, and bestowed two handfuls of
ashes with some smoke from her dhuni upon Shiva
who carried them up. The smoke when sent upwar~
became the sky, and the ashes when strewn
upon the waters formed land. Hence the J ogis
worship only Gorakh Nath and Shiva. By a
process which reminds ~s of the myth of Hephaisto
and Athene§, Gorakh Nath became by a fish
the father of Machhendra Nath, who forthwith
went into the wastes to worship. When Gorakh
Nath was reaproached with his incontinence he
felt that he must seek out a guru of his own, but
finding none better than himself, he bethought
him that his own son 'was fitted for the office
and exclaimed ;Barte khasm,

Yun bhakhe Gorakh abhduta."

Thus saith the ascetiq Gorakh."

He then sought out Machhendra N ath, who
would have fallen at his feet but Gorakh addressed
him as his own guru. This is' how Machhendra
N~th became Gorakh's guru as well as his son.
The Brahmans tell quite a different tale: Bhasmasur, a rakshasa, had long served Shiva, who
in return promised him any boon he might claim.
so he demanded that which when placed on anything would reduce it to ashes. Shiva thereupon
gave him his bangle. Bhasmasur coveted Parbati,
Shiva's wife, and he endeavoured to place the
b~ngle on her husband's head. Shiva fled, pursued

Once, says another legend, the sage Bashisht
recounted the following story to Sri Ram Ohandraji
"My mind was m at ease, and I wandered unti
I came to Bindra Ohal, on which hill I spent a
long period in worship. One day I saw the wife
of Brahma, my fatl,ler coming ~owards me. She
approached and said \my father was worth with her
and I resolved to go tb him, so I went and found a
cave whose mop.th w~s blocked by a stone, Unable
to move it I created \a man by my Brahm-tej
(creative power) and he l'(jkoved the stone. I
I saw a WaNd,
then entered the ca~e, ",herein
I
like the one in which I li{red.! In it were all" the
gods, and I first made a reverence (parnam) to
Brahman and then to all the other gods. But
when I told them of my ehand they warned me
to quit the cave at once, since the day of judgement was at hand because wives were dissatisfied with their husbands. I tdid as they had
bidden me, but meanwhile stillness had prevailed
everywhere and all the earth had turned to water.
Soon a great sound arose from the waters, and
endured for along while but when it had nearly
died away Shakti appeared. I endeavoured to
approach her, but could not even do obeiflance,
and stood like a statue before her. She then cast
a ball into the waters, and i1, made a great sound.
Al3 it died away"she again appeareq.. Thrice she
did this, and the third time Vishnu appeared.
Him she bade to wed her, but he refused and again
she threw a ball upon the waters. Then Brahma
emerged, but 'he too declined her hand and again
she cast a ball. Shiva then appreared in wrathful
mood, and he promised to espouse her, but not yet.
Though-.all these gods were free from maya, never-

theless through it they had appeared, and each
olaimed superiority over the others. Meanwhile
a lotus blossomed on the surface of the waters,
and they agreed that he who should trace
i~ to its root should be deemed the chief.
Neither Bishnu nor Brahma succeeded in this
attempt, but Shiva leaving his body, transformed
himself into an insect and descended through
the stem of the lotus. But his rivals be sought
Shakti to transfigure his body, so as to puzzle him
on his return, and so she took some dirt off her
body and of it, made earrings (Khudal). These
she placed in the ears of Shiva's form, boring
holes in them, and thus re-animated the body.
When it stood up she demanded 'fulfilment of
Shiva's promise, but his form refused to wed her,
so in 1ter '.Vl'ath she threatened to burn it. The
body however, replied tliat her earrings had
made him immortal. Subsequently the earrings
were changed into IDudras, as will be told later
on. The Shakti then asked whose body it was,
and it replied that it was Bhogu-rikh, whereby
J ogis mean one who is immortal and has control
over his senses. Hence Shiva is aleo called Bhogurikh.
Meanwhile Shiva returned, having traced the
lotus to its root. Failing to find his own form he
made for himself a new body* and in that married
Shakti. The descendants of the pair were called
Rudargan, those of Bhogu-rikh being named
Jogijan.
But Shiva's progeny inherited his
fierce temper, and eventually exterminated the
descendants of Bhogu-rikh, who told Shiva that
he, as a jogi, was free from joy or sorrow and was
unconcerned at the quarrel between their children.
But shiva replied: 'Thou art free from maya,
yet dost owe thy existence to it. Do thy work,
I will not interfere'. So Bhogu-rikh began his
task under Shiva's counsel. Initiated by him he
became known as Ude Nath Parbatit and founded
the Jogi panth or "door" (Bashisht's tale woul,q
seem to end here).

Gorakh plays a leading part in the legend of
Guga, and naturally therefore Jogis, both Hindu
and Muhammadan, take offerings made to him,
giving but a small share to the Chuhras; and also
carry his flag, chhari, of peac'ock's feathers, from
house to house in Bhadon.t
The Sidhs, more correctly Siddhs, are properly
speaking saints of exceptional purity of life who
have attained to a semi-divine existence, but who
in the eyes of the vulgar are perhaps little more
than demons who obtained power from Gorakh.
They are especially worshipped in the low hills,§
e.g., in Ambala and Hoshiarpur, in the form of
stones, etc., and under various names. The distinctive emblem of their cult appears to be the
singi, a cylindrical ornament worn on a thread
round the neck. Ghazidas is a Siddh of some
repute near Una: Chanu is said to have been a.
Chamar, and people of that caste feast on goat's
flesh and sing on certain dates to his memory.
Another Siddh is the jathera, or ancestor, Kala
Pir, who is worshipped in the low hills and throughout the eastern Districts generally and mora
particularly, as Kala Mahar, by the Sindhu Jats
as their forebear. His shrine is at Mahar
Samrala but the Sindhus of Khot in Jind have there
~et up a shrine with bricks from .the original tomb
and the.re they, and the Khatis and Lahars too,
worship him. His shrine usually takes the forID
of It mud-pillar under a tree or by 8. pond, and

The mundra: How the Kundal was turned into
a mundra if} explained in the following story:When Bhartari was made a J ogi he was put to a
severe test, J allandaripa was his guru, but he
was also a sadiq or pupil of Gorakh, and his ohief
oOlllpanions were of the Kaplani panth, whence
he was known as Bhartari Kaplani and reckoned
one of the 84 sidhs. One day he said to J allandaripa : "Thou hast put me to a severe test, but
henoeforth the faqirs of this panth will be mostly
men of the world for they will mingle with such
men". Gorakh said that he would be more
pleased with them, and Bharati asked for some
mark to be given them to distinguish them from
worldly people. Accordingly a hole three inches
wide was made in the J ogis ears, and clay mundras were inserted in them. Subsequently the
mundras were made of wood, then of crystal gilt,
then of ivory. By wearing the mundras, a Jogi
becomes inrmortal, as Bhogu-rikh had told Shakti.
When this practice was permitted, two sidhs
Kharkai and Bhuskai began to bore each J ogis
ears, with Gorakh's assent. The latter with these
two sidhs and several other Jogis settled at a
place on the road to Hinglaj in Balochistan, a
place which every J ogi of this panth must visit
if he wishes to be considered a perfect sadbu and
attain yoga. Since then it has been usual to bore
J ogi's ears, but once when the two sidhs tried to
bore the ears of a Jogi who had visited that place
they found that they headled as fast as they bored
holes 41 them, so they gave up the attempt, and
Gorakh exclaimed that the pilgrim was 'Aughar'.
Thenceforth Aughars do not have their ears bored
and form a body distinct frQm the other J ogis.
J ogi Nature worship : The J ogis claim, inter
alia, power to transmut~ any metal into gold or
silver. In the time of Altamsh, says one legend,
a J ogi named Dina Nath begged a boy sitting in
a shop with a heap of coppir coin to give him a
few pieces. The boy said the money was not his,
but his father's, and he gave the Jogi food. The
jogi prayed to Vishnu for power to reward the
boy. Then he melted-down the copper and turned

REPRINTS

t.he mas~ into gold by means of charms and a
powder. Altamash heard of tbe occurrence and
witnessed the Jogi's powers, but ·the latter declined to accept any of the gold he had made, so
it was sent to the mint and coined, with his name
as well as that of Altamsh upon it. J ogis allege
tha.t these 'Dinanathi' gold mohars are still to be
found.
Similarly the Jogis claim power over hailstorms,
and in Sialkot the rathbana* is a Jogi who can
check a hailstorm or divert in into waste land.
The connection between J ogis and snake worship
is naturally close one. In some places J ogis are
said to eat snakes a kind of ritualistic cannibalism
and the snake is often styled jogi, just as the
parrot is designated panditt.
The cults of the J ogis contain,strong elements of
nature worship which finds expression in the
names assllmed by them after initiati~n. Such are
Nim-Nath:j:, Kanak-nath (wheat), \ Nag-nath
(snake),
Tota-nath (Parrot).
The J ogis hold ever},-thing made of \earth in
great respect, whence the saying -Mitti ka asan,
mitti ka basan, mitti ka sarhana, mitti ka bana'T~e earthen asan (carpet), the earthen pitcher,
the earthen pillow and the earthen woof'.
The J ogi J aneo: The J ogis generally wear a
jane~ of black wool, which is made by certain
members of the order, not by any member, nor
by a Brahman. It is 9 cubits long, made of
3 strands each, woven of 8 threads on a bobbin,
and plainted into a bobbin-thread, like an English
braid necklace§. Round the waist J ogis wear a

The J ogi funeral rites : A dying J ogi is made to
sit cross-legged. Mer death the corpse is washed
by the deceased's fellow Jogis, a langotl tied
round its waist and ashes smeared over it. A
coffin is then made, if means permit, but a poor
jogi is simply wrapped in a blanket and carried by
two men o~ two poles, and the body thrown into
a river. A wealthy jogi is, however, placed. on a
wooden chauln shaped like a palanquin, and upon
this flowers are cast. The procession to the grave
is called sawari and is headed by horses and bands
playing music_ The grave is made deep, with a
spacious niche like that in Muhammadan graves
and the body placed in it cross-legged and facing
the north*. T~e Jogi's bairagan is placed
before him, with!. a gourd full of water on his
right, a loin-clot~ a kanak or staff of Mahadeo,
a loaf of wheaten:, flour, and two earthen plates
one fuU of water, ~he other of rice and milk. An
earthern potsherd i~ .also' l!laced on his head. Then
a mound is raised l over,~he grave,t and all the
J ogis wash their ha'nds with water supplied by the
deceased's disciples. They;' then bathe and the
disciples give them sweets. On the third day they
are also fed (churma alone being given if the disoiples are poor). Later on the shradh is, if possible
perform.ed thus:-,ogis are invited and keep
a vigil all night. About a pahr before dawn they
are fed with fish, or pakauras (vegetables coated
with baisan or paste of powdered gram fried
in mustard oil), or khir, i.e., rice, boiled in milk
gram and ghungnian, or pilao, or rice, wine, flesh,
fruit, etc. Seven thrones or gaddis are now erected
1;0 : (i) the Pir, (ii) Jognis, (iii) Sakhya or witness,
(iv) Bir, (v) the Bhandari of Guru Gorakh Naths,
(vi) Guru Gorakh Nath, and (vii) to Neka. Mantras
are then repeated, and clothes : gold, silver and
\jopper : a cow and earth given away in charity.
The wake is now attended only by J ogis but for.l1l",rly men of all classes, even Muhammadans,
used to take part in it. Lastly, after all these cereI!lOmes, a council (pindhara) of J ogis is held and
one of the deceased's disciples is elected Guru or
Bir 1\lahan, three kinds of food, Puri, Kachouri
and Pilao being distributed. The deceased's clothes
and the coffin are given to the Kotwals, or bankias,
or else to Jangam faqirs. As the .Jogi is not burnt
his bones cannot be sent to the Ganges, so his

nails are ·removed and taken to Hardwar. The
samadh of a Jogi may be of earth or brick, and
belpattar (leaves) are strewn over it. On it a lamp
is also kept burning for 10 days, flowers and
water being placed near it and conch being blown.
Rioe balls are given in the name of the deoeased
for 10 days as among other Hindus. On the 10th
day clothes are washed and on the 13th kirya
karam ceremony is performed. The ceremonies
are the same as among Hindus.
The following story is told to account for the
fact that Jogis bury their dead: In Gorakh's time
there arose a dispute between the Hindus and the
Muhammadans, the latter saying they were masters
of the earth and of all the living and the dead.
Gorakh sat on the ground, placing all his food,
etc., by his side, and bade the earth yeild to him,
if he too had a share in it. It opened and Gorakh
sank into it, and so J ogis usually bury their dead.
Initiation : In theory an Hindu can become
a J ogi, but in praotice only those of the twioeborn castes are admitted into the order. In theory
caste is abandoned upon entering it, and as
marriag~ is, in theory, forbidden, no question as
to caste can arise in connection with it. But as
marriage is in practice tolerated the original caste
is preserved in praotice for matrimonial purposes,
though in theory all Jogis are caste less. Further
there is a tendency to a void marriage in the same
panth, as all the memhers of a panth are in theory
spiritually akin. Within the order there is in
theory equality and no restrictions are placed
upon e~ting, drinking or smoking together, but
even a Hindu of high caste who joins the panth
of J alandhar Nath is excluded by other panths.
Moreover, the theoretical equality does not e~end
to the women, as the J ogi does not allow his women
folk to eat with mm. Women of every panth may,
however, eat t06'ether.
A would be disciple is dissuaded from becoming
a J ogi, the hardships of the life being impressed
upon him. If he persists he is made to fast for
two or three days. Mter this, a knife is driven into
the earth and the novice is made to swear' by it ;(i)

not to engage in trade;

(ii) not to take employment;

defiled as they take offerings made at death, and the Kanets and higher castes will not drink with them.

Up to a certain point the Jogi initiatory rites
fe&emble those of the Saniasis. The choti of the

The guru-mantar is then communicated,

now becomes a nath, certain set phrases (not

J ogis smear ~8hes on their naked bodies as

but the ashes appear to symbolize their death to
the world, like the kafni.
We may thus &afoly distinguish three stages in
a Jogi's initiation. At first he is a chela (pupil or
candidate), then an augha.r or novioot and finally
a darshani, vulg. kanphatta.,§ (or split-eared).
An Aughar is not entitled to aU tho privileges of
the sect, e.g., at a feast he only receives half the
portion of a Kanphatta. A Jogi who is fully initiated certainly loses all rights of inheritance in
his natural family, but it is doubtful whether.an
Aughar, would do so. It is also not clear whether
initiation involves the loss of property already

vested in the initia.te, but pretsllmably it would do
so.
The derivation of Aughar is obscure. The
grade or order, huwever we regard it, does hot
a.ppear to be connected with the Agbori or Ghorpanthi6 who are cannibal faqirs of a singularly
repulsive type\\. The Anghars of Kirana in Jhang
are of good reput3 and retain large jagirs granted

ochre-coloured turban over which is twisted a black
n6t-work of thread covered with gold. The mahant
is styled pir, and once elecUld may never again
de.sctJlld the hill.
To these three degrees may perhaps be added a
fourth, that of mahatma, a dignity hardly alluded
to in the accounts rendered of the sect. A J ogi
who attains to great spiritual eminence is, exempt
from wearing mundra.s, the janeo, and so on.
After initiation a J ogi may apparent.ly select _
the function which he is to fulfil. Thu~\he may
become a militant member of the sect, vowed to
celibacy and styled Nang~, Naga Nadi, Nihang,
Kanphara or Kanphatta.
\
Or he may relapse arid, breaking bis vow of
celibacy, become a secular J ogi, designated BindiNagi,i Sanyogi (Samayogi), Gh arb ari or Grishiti.
Lastly, tho initiate Jogi may join one of the
va.rious panths or orders. These panths are in
theory limited to twelve in number, but in reality
they number many more than twelve.

Raja Bhartari was the son of Raja Bhoj, king

seeing the flower still fresh knew that the Raja
only doubted her love for him and in grief at
his mistrust killed herself. When she was carried
out to the burning ground the Raja evinced great
grief, and Gorakh appeared. Brea.king this chipi, ~
the saint walked round it, weeping and, Bhartari
.asked him why he grieved. Gorakh answered that
he could get the Raja a thousand queens, but never
a vessel like the one he had just broken, and he
showed him a hundred ranis as fair as Pingla,
but each of tbem said: 'Hold aloof! Art thou mad?
Noone knows how often we bave been thy motheI's
or sisters or wives.'. Hearing these words Bhart!lori's grief was moderated and be made Gorakh his

hinds with a single stag. He failed to kill the stag,

he was to his queens, but the Raja said he, a
Kshatriya, could not kill a hind. So the hind who
had I!poken bade the stag meet the Raja's arrow,

Upon no topic is our information so confused,

The Abha-panthi is probably identical with the

The Aghori, Ghori or Aghor-panthi is an order
which smears itself with excrement, drinks out of
a tuman skull, and occasionally digs up the recently buried body of a ohild and eats it; thus
carrying out the principle that nothing is common
or unclean to its extreme logioal conclusion.
The Ai-panth is a well-ln:l.Own order, said to be

naga or naked for ever. So to this day his descendants are called Nagas.
Another account

The Brahma ka order appears to be the same
as the Sat-Nath.
The Darya-nathi order is chiefly found in the
west, especially trans-Indus. It possesses gadCtis
at Makhad on the Indus, in Kohat and even in
Quetta.
The Dhaj-panthi order is found in or at least
reported from Peshawar and in Ambala. It may be
that the order derives its name from dhaj meaning
flag. Mr. Maclagan mentions the Dhaj-panthi
as followers of Hanuman.
The Tahqiqat gives
Dhaja-panthi as \he form of the nam!l.
The Dharmnathi order is widely spread, but

dation ascribed to ~ Raja Dharm.

The Kaplani or Kapil-panthi order ascribes

Pal, Kapal Muni's disciple, and is thus cousin to

The Kaya-nathi or Kayan nathi is an offshoot
of the Ganga-nathi. But in Dera Ghazi Khan it is
said that they received their name from Pir Ratn
N ath who made an image out of the dirt of his own

The Kanthar or Khantar order owes its origin
to Ganesha. In Ambala it is said to be endogamous.
Lachhman Nath's order is said in Hoshiarpur
to be also known as the Darbari Nath Tilla Bal
Gondai, but in Amritsar is said to be the same as
the Natesri (as in Maclagan, §55).

disciples of the Devi

The Man Manthi appear to be identical with
the Man Nath, returned from Peshawar, and the
Manathi or Mannati in Jhelum who ascribe their
foundation to Raja Rasalu. Mr. Marlagan mentions the Man-nath as followers of Rasalu, §55.

The Mekhla dhari is a class or order which is
returned from Ambala and its name is said to mean
wearer of the taragi.
The Natesri order appears to have no representatives in the Punjab but see above under Lachh~an N ath's order.
The Nim Nathia is distinct from the order founded by Paras Nath q.v. It is said to be also called
Gaplani or Kisgai.
The Papanth appears to be also called Panathi
or Panpatai, a sub-order founded by J alandhar as
a disciple of Mahadeo.
The Pagal ap:gears to 1)e identical with the
Rawal-Ghalla.
The Paras Nath order is sometimes shown as
half an order, the Rawals being its other half. But
Paras Nath was one of Machhendra's two sons
and he founded an order which soon split up into
two distinct schools, (i) the Puj-who are celibate
but live in houses and .observe none of the rules
observed by (ii) the Sartoras, who always wear a
cloth over the mouths, strain water before drinking
it, never kill aught that has life: further they never
build houses, but lead, a wandering life, eating
only food cooked by others, and smoking from a
chilam, never from a Hukkah. That these two suborders are both Jains by religion, if not by sect, is
perfectly obvious, and it is indeed expressly said
that this Paras Nath is he whom the Jains rever.
The Ram-ke, or Ram Chandra-ke, panth was
founded by Ram Nath, a disciple of Santokh Nath,
and had its head-quarters on the Godawari till
it was replaced there by the Dharm-nathi. It
appears to be sometimes ascribed to Ram Chandra,
but erroneously so.

The J ogis: The J ogis or Yogi, properly so
clltlled, is a follower cf the Yoga system of philosophy founded by Patanjali, the main characteristics of which are a belief in the power of man
over nature by means of austeritie!l and the occult
influence of the will. The purer forms of this
teacbing pass, as may be easily imagined, into.a
theosophic creed whioh is independent of religion.

formerly large grants of revenue attached to monastery, but these were abolished some years ago
when the Mohant was accused of murderjng his
rival for the gaddi. The Jogis of Tilla are not a!J. estimable body, but are held in considerable reputation even by Musalmans, and they have Hindu
disciples away beyond the Afghan border. The
Oghars in Kirana are better repute and still retain
large jagirs granted'to them in Sikh times. They
are distinguished by an ochre-coloured turban
over which is twisted a black net-work of thread
covered with gold; their Mohants are known as
Pirs, and a Mohant once elected may never descend the hill. The name Phul Swami in our returns is said to refer to a Jogi of Tilla, while that
of Jiwan-das refers to the Mohant at Kirana.
The prevalence of the J ogis on and beyond the
frontier'is a matter worthy of notice, more especially
in connection with the supposed relation of the
Jogis to ancient Buddhism. The Gorakhatri at
Peshawar, which Sir A. Cunningham identifies
with -an old Buddhist monastery, is connected by
legend with ~he name 9f Gorftkhtlath, and was in
former times a haunt of Jogis. "I had heard," says
Babar, "the .fame of the Gorkhatri which is one of
the holy places of the Jogis of the Hindus who
com~ from great distances to cut oft their hair and

shave their beards at this Gorkhatri," and Abulfazl writes: "Here is a temple called Corkhatri,
a place of religious resort, especially for Jogis."
At present the chief Jogi saint of this part
of the country is Pir Rattan Nath. There are
shrines connected with his name at Peshawar,
Jalalabad and Kabul, and the gaddi-nashines or
incumbents at each of these is termed Gosain.
Even the fanatical Mahomadans of these parts are
said to have a firm belief in the powers of thi , Jogi.
At the Oensus 439 persons from Rawalpindi and
201 from Hazara returned themselves as his
followers.
Gorakhnath is also worshipped so far field as at

CS7. Jogi Tenets and Customs: 'l'he Jogls profess
to hold by the following sacred works, namely,
the Gorakh Shank~~ }:6 Gorakh Manjri, GorakhShabodatta, Atam-boddh, Brahm-gyan, and
Gorakhpanjratan, most of which are, I believe,
unknown to fame. The.Jogis as a class are ignorant,
and very few of them are scholars like the
Sanniiisis; but they are very often found singing on
the guitar and have a number of oral traditions
and songs which Illight throw some light on their
origin and history.
They are not drawn from any particular castes.
Anyone, even a Mahomedan, may become a Jogi.
The J ogi is a Shaiva, and can eat and drink liquor.
He crries out af! he begs, the name of "Alakh,"
-Alakshma, God the Un-marked, the Formless.
The J ogis are not supposed to marry, and they
are recruited mainly by the entrance into the order
of boys whom their parents have promised to Jogis
on condition that the latter should by some
means procure them children. One section of the
order, indeed, called Samyogi, is said to marry,
but these men are looked on as outcastes by the
rest of the J ogis. The J ogi wears clothes of a brickdust colour or none at all, smearing his body with
earth. The familiarity of the Jogi with earth is
exemplified in the saying, "Mitt! ke iisau, mitti
ka basan, mitti kii sarbena, mitti ka bana;" "Earth
for seat, earth for basin, earth for pillow, earth for
raiment." The Jogis either do not wear the janeo
(sacred thread) or wear one of an extraordinary
size and make. Like the Sanniasis they do not wear
the scalp-lock, generally platting their hair with
ropes of black wool over their heads into what is
caned the Jata. Like the Sanniasis, too, they bury,
and do not burn, their dead. The Jogi is buried sitting crosslegged facing the north, in a tomb which
has a recess like those of Mahomedans. A gourd,
a crutch, some bread, earthenware, etc., are placed
by him, a.nd a marhi or samadh is erected over the
grave.
There are many things which point to a nonHindu origin for the Sannias'is and J ogis. The
Hindu 'Years a scalp-lock, carries the sacred thread,
>burns his dead, and, generally speaking, abstains
from flesh and wine. The J ogis and SanniaSls do
none of these things. The Jogis, too, as I have
already noticed, are remarkably prevalent in the
Peshawar and Kabul direction where Buddhism
was once so strong. And the names of their twelve
Naths bear some correspondence with those of
the Jain Tirthankeras. There are legends, too,
which connect Gorakh Nath in a special way with
Nepal, and the panth of Jalandhar Nath is often

termed the Pa-nanth, from the fact that is members in place of "Nath" adopt after their names the
termination "P a" which is the Tibetan equivalent
for the more familiar "Wela." The origin of these
two peculiar orders-the Jogis and Sanniasis-is
a matter of some interest, and can scarcely be said,
so far as I know, to have been properly cleared up
as yet.

Julaha, fem..-i, syn, safed-baf. The weavers
proper, of which the J ulaha, as he is called in the
east, and the Paoli as he is called in the villages
ot the West, is the type, are an exceedingly numerous an,d important artisan class, more especially
in the western Districts where no weaving is done
bv the leather-working or scavenger castes. It is
very possible that the Julaha is of aboriginal extraction. rndeed Sir James Wilson who had, in the
old Sirsa district, unequalled opportunities of com.paring different sections of the people, is of opinion
that the JulahJ.s and Chamlj-rsare probably the same
by origin, the distinction between them having
arisen from divergence of occupation. Be this as it
may, there is no doubt that the present position
of the two is widely dissimilar. The Julaha does
not work in im.pure leather, he eats no carrion,
he touches no carcases, and he is recognised by, both
Hindu and Musalman as' a fellow-believer and admitted to religious equality. In a word, the Chamar
is a menial, the J ulaha an artisan. The real fact
seems to be that the word JUlaha, form the Persian

Julah, a ball of thread, the equivalent Hindi term
bei?g ~anti, is the name of the higbest occupation
ordinarily open to the outcast section of the community. Thus we find Koli-Julahas, ChamarJulahas, Mochi-Julahas Ramdasi-JuJahas, and so
forth: and it is probable that after a few generations
these men drop the prefix which denotes their low
origin and become J ulahas pure and simple. The
weaver app"ears to be called Golah in Peshawar and
Kasbi in Hazara.
The J ulaha proper is scantily represented in the
80uth-east Punjab, where his place is taken by the
Koli* or Chamar-Julaha and Dhanak; and he is
bardly known in the Derajat, where probably the
Jat does most of the weaving. In the rest of the
Province he con~titutes some 3 to 4 per cent of the
total population. \He is generally a Hindu in Kangra
and Delhi, and eften Hindu in Karnal, Ambal\lo
and Hoshiarpur; but on the whole some 92 per cent
of the Julahas ate Musalban. Sikhs are few in
number.

~
The Julaha conJines himself almost wholly to
weaving. He is not a true vil).age menial, being paid
by the piece and not by customary dues. He is
perhaps the most troublesome of the artisan
\cl,asses. Like the shoe-maker of Europe, he follows a
wholly sedentary Qycupation, and in the towns at
least is one of the most turbulent classes of the
community. There is a proverbial saying: "How
should a weaver be patient1" Indeed the contrast
between the low social standing and the obtrusive
pretentiousness of the class is often used to point
a proverb: "A weaver by trade, and his name is
Fatah Khan ('victorious chief')" "Lord preserve
us! The ,weaVer is going out hunting!" "Himself
a weaver, and he has a Saiyad for his.servant!"
"What! Pathans the bond servants of weavers!"
and so forth.
The J ulaha.sub-divisions are exceedingly numerous, but the names of most of the larger ones are
taken from dominant land-owing tribes. Some of
the largest are-Bhattis who are very widely distributed Khokhars chiefly found west of Lahore;
J anjuas and Awans in the Rawalpindi division;
Sindhus in the Central Punjab, and the Jaryals
in Kangra. The Kabirhansi arl) found in Ambala

and Kangra, and apparently this word has become
a true tribal name and now includes M\lSalman
Julahas. It is derived from the great Bhagat Kabir
of Benares who was himself a J ulaha, and whose
teaching most of the Hindu Julabas profess to
follow. The eastern Julahas are said to be divided
into two great sections, Deswale, or those of the
country, and Tel, the later being supposed to be
descended from a Julaha who married a Teli
woman. The'latter are socially inferior to the former.
In the Jumna districts there are also a Gangapuri
(1 Gangapari) and a Multani section, the former
being found only in the Jumna valley and the latter
on the border of the Malwa.
Further west we find the Muhamadan Julahas
divided in the several groups, mostly territorial,
e.g., in Jind we have the Jangli, Deswali, Bajwarya and Parya sub-castes. But the Nabha.
version gives six groups, four territorial, viz.,
Jangla, Pawadhre ('of the Pawadh'), Bagri a.nd
Multani (these two latter are not found in the
State), one called Pare and a sixth called Mochia.
which is nominated from the Mochis. The fom
groups found in Jind all eat and smoke together.
The Janglis are found in the Jangal tract of tahsil
Sangrur. They have hereditary Pirs, who are
Sayyids. In adopting a Pir a murid (disciple)
takes a cup of sliarbat from his hand and drinks
it, believing that by so doing he will attain
to Bahisht (Paradise). They revere their Pirs,
give them a rupee and ~ wrapper when they come
to their house and entertain them well. The J angli
gots are those of the Jats and Rajputs, and it is
said that they were converted during the reign of
Aurangzeb. Some of them still retain their Brahman
parohits and give them money at weddings.
They only avoid their own got in marriage.
The Pare in Nabha follow the Muhammadan Law
as to marriage, whereas the other five groups avoid
four gots in marriage, like Hindust.
The Muhammadan Julahss are said to he very
strict observers of the Id-ul-fitr, just as the Qassabs (butchers) hold the Id-ul-zuha in special
esteem, while the Kanghigarans affect the Shabi-barat and Sayyids the Muharram.

fled to Kashmir. The Kambohs of Bijnor also
trace their origin to the trans-Indus country, and
Mr. Purser accept~ this tradition as evidently true.
They are s.aid by some to be ancient inhabitants of
Persia, and the Kamal Kambohs trace their origin
from Garh Ghazni; but the fact that 40 per cent
of them are Hindus and 23 per cent, Sikhs is conclusive against their having had any extra-Indian
origin, unless at a very remote period. Arains and
Kambohs are commonly supposed to be closely
related: Indeed in Montgomery a man appears to
be called Arain if he is Musalman and Kamboh if
Hindu.t But that this is not always the case is
evident from the fact of ,a very co~iderable proportion of the Kambohs of Amritsar, Lahore,
Ferozepur, Patlala, Nabha and Maler Kotlahaving
returned themselves as Musalmans, although
Musalman Arains are also numerous in those tracts.
In Jullundur the village of Bhalowal is owned
partly by Kambohs and partly by Araina, both
being Musalman. It is perhaps doubtful whether the
supposed relationship has any further basis than
the fact that ,they both came from the west, and
are both of much the same social standing and agricultural repute. It is highly probable that the nucleus
of the Arain caste was a group of Hindu Kambohs
converted to Islam. Thus in Jullundur the Gaure,
Hande and MOnll clans are found in both castes,
and in Montgomery several of their clan names
are identical. It 'is said by some that the chief
distinction is tnat the Kambohs take money for
their daughters, while the Arains do not. Bu~the
social standing of the Kamboh is on the whole
superior to that of the Arain, and very markedly
so where the latter is a vegetable-grower. The

Kamboh, moreover, is not a mere agriculturist.
He not infrequently engages in trade and even takes
service in the army or in offices or even as a private
serv.ant, while his wife not infrequently lends money
even where he is a mere husbandman; and under
Akbar a Kamboh general called Shahbaz Khan
commanded 5,000 men and distinguished himself
greatly in Bengal.t
Musalman Kambohs held Sohna in Gurgaon some
centuries ago; and the tombs and mosques that
they have left show that they must have enjoyed
a considerable position. The military, mercantile,
and clerkly Kambohs are said to be distinguished
as Qalmi or "men of the pen," and not to intermarry with the agricultural or Khaki section of the
caste. But thi~ is probably a mere social custom
and not a caste rule. The Kambohs do not seem
to bear as high a character for honesty as they do
for skill. There is a Persian proverb current in the
United Provinces; "The Mghans, the Kambohs,
and the Kashmiris; aU three are rogues (badzat),"
and in Kamal Mr. Benton described them as "notoriously deceitful and treacherous." On the
other hand Sardar Gurdial Singh states, it is not
known on what authority that "during the reign
of t~rror in India, it was the Kambohs who were
trusted by the rich bankers for carrying their
cash in the disguise of faqirs." The Kambohs are
said to be exceptionally numerous in Meerut.
Their location under the hills lends some slight
snpport to their tradition of origin from Kashmir.

State of Bahawalpur, but they offer some points

Some popular accounts of the origin of the Dame
Kamboh follow:
(1) Once a powerful Raja of the solar race,
whose capital was at Ajudhia, marched thence to
Derat and having killed Parmar, its Raja, took
possession of his kingdom. He founded Warangar
and his son founded another town, which he named
Dejapur, and the cities of Lambni and Gajni.
The latter was his capital, and lay near the city
of Kambay, the ,peninsula south of Guzerat.
At the Solono festival when Ihe was performing
religious rites he Jas attacked by an enemy who
had conspired with\his parobj.t, his city was plun- \
dered and its peoRle mass)1cred. Of those who
escaped some fled t~ Sa~ana along the Ghaggar,
passfug by Jaipur an'p. Sirh~nd on their way, thence
spread over the coup-try between the Jumna and
the Sutlej, and after wandering through'the
country watered by the Sutlej and Beas scattered
over the whole Punjab. Others reached Multan
~a Sind and thence spread into Montgomery.
They are called Kampohsl beqause they came from
Gajni, neal KambaYlOthers assert that the name
is a corruption of Kambudh (men of little intellect)
because they did not take up arms on the Solono
day, but preferred to die.
(2) Raja Sodakhsh of Kamboj of the Solar race
and a descendant of the god chander Burman
sided with the Kauravas in their fight with the
Pandavas. He perished with nearly all his men in
the battlefield, and those who escaped settled in
Nabha and came to- be called Kamboji whence
Kamboh.
(3) Kamboh is said to be compounded of Kai and
anboh, ~nd the tribe is said to be descended from
the Kai dynasty of Persia, to which the emperors
Kaik~us, Kaikhuslo, Kailqtbad, Kai-Lehrashab
and Darius all belonged. When they migrated to
the Punjab they came to be called Kai-ambohs or
Kambohas.
(4) Hazrat Abdulla, son of Zuber, was sent with
a large army to conquer Persia, where he settled
and built many huts on the banks of the river.
The ~ersians could not understand ~heir tongue
(ArabIC), so they became kam-go or taciturn.
Zuber's anny comprised men of many beliefs.
In time their settlements were destroyed and the
Kamgos fled.

The first story is the one naturally favoured by
the Kambohs themselves a d the fact remains
that the Solono festival is not observed by them,
because they regard it as inauspicious. The author
of the Aina Tarikhnama and Gur Tirath Sangra
has given an account of the Kambohs and assigns
their origin to the Kambojas, but against this it
may be urged that the Kambohs(i) do not observe the Salono or tie the Rakhri
on it:
(ii) at the phera their parohits proclaim Garh
Gajni or Ghaggar Bas as their regional
home;
(iii) that their gots corr spond with those of
the Brahmans and Chhatris:
(iv) that they perform the parojan or bandhan
ceremony:
(v) that they worship weapons at the Dasehra
and wear them at weddings; and
(vi) that they cut the jand tree a.nd sacrifice a
he-goat at a marriage.
The only point which merit, notice "in these
folk-etymologies is the allusion to Sodaksh (Sudakshina), king of Kamboja, a territory which lay
under the hills, which now form the northern
border of the Attock and Rawalpindi Districts,
from the Indus to the Jhelum. That king, according
to the Mahabharata, joined the Kauravas with
an army containing Yavanas and Shakas. But
Kamboja also appears to have been the name of a
tribe. These facts are in accord with the tradition
that the Kamboh.came 'from Kashmir, but beyond
that there is absolutely nothing but the resemblance
in the names to enable us to identify the Kambohs
with the Kambojas. How their gots can be said to
correspond'with those of the Brahmans or Chhatris
is not clear. The Kambohs have very few large subdivisions. The nine largest are- Dahut, Jaura,
Sande, J arumun, Jhande, Thind, J ausan, Mahrok,
Unmal.
The Kambohs are by religion Hindu, especially
in the east, Sikh, especially in the Sikh Districts,
while some are Jain and a great many are Muhammadans. The latter are in Lahore described as
hardly d4;tinguishable from the Arains, but the
Sikh Kamboh is better than either, being equal
to the Arain in Industry, but more enterPrising
and more provident. He matches the Arain as &
market gardener and is not inferior to the Sidhu
J at in general farming though he is smaller in physique and less intelligent than the Jat. The Sikh
Kambohs in the Chenah Oolonr n\lnlbe:fe4 OTIf
